FINAL MANAGEMENT REPORT:
The final management report discusses the technical advancement made on the project.
Overall Objectives
The overall objectives of this work are to investigate systematically the effects of design and
operating variables on the fluid dynamics of spouted fluidized bed coaters, to evaluate the reported
dimensionless groups as scaling parameters and to quantify their sensitivity, to advance the fundamental
understanding of the hydrodynamics, to establish a reliable scale-up methodology of the spouted bed
TRISO fuel particles coater, and to develop an on-line non-invasive measurement technique based on
gamma ray densitometry that can be installed and used for process monitoring to ensure proper
performance and operation, and to facilitate the developed scale-up methodology. The work will use CT,
CARPT, gas tracer, optical probe, pressure transducers, and CFD. The effects of particle-particle
interactions and the thermal expansion of the gas phase on the flow field of the coater will be accounted
for.
Proposed Scale-up Approach
The performance of the coater and the quality of the coating applied to the fuel kernels are
essentially determined by the hydrodynamics, flow field, and mixing of the spouted bed. Based on the
results and findings reported in the literature (He et al., 1997, Mabrouk et al., 2005, Schweitzer et al.,
2001; Minato et al., 1994; Cassanello et al., 1993; Djeridane et al., 1998 and others), and based on using a
similar approach as our newly developed scale-up methodology for bubble and slurry bubble columns,
(Al-Dahhan and Shaikh, 2005), we propose that the hydrodynamics similarity in spouted fluidized beds
due to changes in conditions and/or scale (size) can be maintained by also maintaining the similarity of
the following hydrodynamic parameters (the effects of design and operating variables, including particle-
particle interactions and gas phase thermal expansion, are considered within them)
1. Radial profile of solids or voidage holdups (and/or cross sectional distribution of the solids or
voidage holdup in terms of mean, variance, and shape of their probability density function
distribution) at selected dimensionless heights (e.g., spouted bed, fountain developed region, and
fountain gas/solids disengagement region) and/or in general along the dimensionless bed height.
This includes maintaining the similarity of the dimensionless spout diameter along the
dimensionless height of the bed.
2. Dimensionless fountain height.
Advanced non-invasive measurement techniques will be used to examine and validate this
proposed hypothesis. The key dimensionless groups, reported in the literature mentioned earlier,
(Glicksman, 1984; Glicksman et al., 1993; He et al., 1997; Minato, 1994; IAEA, 1997, etc.) will be then
evaluated as scaling factors, and their sensitivity will be quantified and/or new one(s) will be identified
that can be properly correlated to predict the measured radial profiles of the solids and dimensionless
fountain heights in spouted fluidized bed coaters. The obtained data and findings will be used to evaluate
and validate the CFD models and the available closures (with consideration to account for particle-
particle interactions and thermal expansion of the gas). Successful validation of CFD will help facilitate
the scale-up, design, and process optimization of the real TRISO fuel particle coaters by searching for the
conditions that match these parameters (mentioned in 1 and 2 above). The development of the on-line
gamma ray densitometry technique aims at measuring these parameters and identifying the coater flow
regime to facilitate the assurance of the coater’s proper performance and its scale-up hydrodynamics
similarity.

Proposed Tasks
The following is an outline of the tasks proposed to accomplish the overall objectives set for the project:
Task 1: Development of on-line and non-invasive technique for process monitoring and
measurements
1.1 Develop a technique based on gamma ray densitometry that can be used on-line (on real TRISO
coating processes) to identify the spouting flow regime and to measure the radial profiles of solids
and voidage holdup, dimensionless spout diameter, and the dimensionless fountain height along the
dimensionless bed height. Develop flow regime identifiers for all spouted fluidized bed flow
regimes, based on signal processing of the measurements from the developed gamma ray
densitometry technique. In our laboratory, various time-series analyses have been used (such as
statistical, spectral, autocorrelation, Hurst, regularity of cycle time, chaos, wavelengths, amplitude,
and cycle strength (Al-Dahhan and Shaikh, 2005)). Also, a new flow regime identifier was developed
for identifying bubbly and turbulent flow regimes in a bubble/slurry bubble column, based on gamma
ray densitometry measurement (Shaikh and Al-Dahhan, 2006). The same approach will be used for
this task. The developed technique will be validated and tested against gamma ray CT. Implementing
such a successful development on commercial TRISO fuel particle coaters can be achieved by
utilizing commercially available NGD.
1.2 Assess the implementation of the newly developed advanced non-invasive and on-line technique
(Task 1.1 above) on the reported TRISO coaters (Shehata and Al-Dahhan, 2006, feasibility study
mentioned above). This effort includes assessment of its installation, how it can be utilized as an on-
line monitoring system, safety issues, and its economic analysis.
Task 2: CT, pressure drop fluctuation, and optical probe measurements and investigations
2.1 Identify two sets of particles and conditions according to the work of He et al. (1997) that have been
experimentally demonstrated to provide match and mismatch in radial solids holdup profiles and
fountain dimensionless height. Use CT and gamma ray densitometry (or NGD) to evaluate the
selected conditions from He et al. (1997), and adjust them as necessary to ensure the match and
mismatch in these parameters.
2.2 For the selected two sets of particles, investigate the effects of scale (coater size), design, and
operating conditions (including thermal expansion of the gas phase) outlined earlier on the parameters
listed below using CT, NGD, optical probes, and pressure transducers along the bed for pressure drop
and absolute pressure fluctuation measurements:
- Cross-section distribution and radial profile of the solids and voidage holdup along the bed height
- Dimensionless spout diameter along the bed height
- Dimensionless fountain height and ratio of the average fountain apogee to the bed diameter
- Ratio of the average fountain diameter to the bed surface diameter
- Average amplitude of slugs, measured as vertical displacement of the bed over the gas inlet relative
to the static bed height
- Ratio of the maximum apogee to the average apogee of fountain ejecta
- Longitudinal pressure profiles
The above mentioned time-series analyses (in Task 1) will be used to quantify the measurements of
these techniques at various conditions.
Task 3: CARPT Measurements and Investigations
For the identified sets of conditions that provide the best match and best mismatch of the
solids/voidage holdups, radial profiles, dimensionless spout diameters, and dimensionless fountain height,
investigate the detailed 3D solids flow field, velocity, and turbulent parameters using CARPT. The
following parameters will be measured and studied: 3D flow pattern, 3D velocity components, 3D
turbulent parameters (shear stresses, normal stresses, kinetic energy, granular temperature, eddy
diffusivity), particle-particle interaction, circulation time distribution, stagnant zones, local residence
time, solids flux mass balance (solids fluxes upward and downward), solids Lagrangian trajectories,
solids mass circulation rate, dimensionless measure of cycle time, and average amplitude of slugs
measured as the vertical displacement of the bed over the gas inlet relative to the static bed height
5

(Bhusarapu, 2005; Luo, 2005). In addition to these parameters, the following can be obtained from
CARPT as well, and will be compared with those obtained in Task 2: dimensionless fountain height,
dimensionless spout diameter, 3D distribution and radial profile of solids/voidage (Mabrouk et al., 2005),
ratio of the average fountain diameter to bed surface diameter, ratio of the average fountain height to the
bed diameter, ratio of the maximum apogee to the average apogee of fountain ejecta, and average
amplitude of slugs measured as the vertical displacement of the bed over the gas inlet relative to the static
bed height. The following sub-tasks will be conducted:
3.1 Based on the detailed local hydrodynamic measurements above, evaluate the proposed scale-up
methodology and the conditions that assist in maintaining the hydrodynamics similarity. If needed,
readjust the conditions to maintain such similarity. In this case, a modification to the proposed
methodology by identifying another key hydrodynamic parameter(s) to be matched will be sought in
the light of the results and findings.
3.2 Evaluate the reported dimensionless groups mentioned earlier as scaling factors and/or identify
additional groups that can be properly correlated to predict the proposed scale-up parameters of the
scale-up methodology. These dimensionless groups will be first used as a preliminary attempt to
identify the mimic conditions for the match and the mismatch of the TRISO fuel coater conditions.
Such development is considered as a first attempt, since along with the accomplishment of the
following tasks, validated CFD models and closures will be used (if possible or successful) to
facilitate the identification of mimic conditions for hydrodynamic similarity of the AGR fuel kernels
coater.
3.3 Investigate the effects of design and operating conditions (including thermal expansion of the gas
phase) on the detailed hydrodynamic parameters listed above, using the conditions outlined earlier
and/or adjusted based on the results and findings.
3.4 Reassess the ability of the evaluated dimensionless groups as scaling factors (Task 3.2) taking into
account thermal expansion of the gas phase and its impact on hydrodynamics similarity. Modify the
dimensionless if possible
Task 4: Gas dynamic measurements and investigations
For the conditions studied in Tasks 2 and 3, characterize and investigate the gas dynamics and its
dispersion, using our newly developed technique based on the measurement of the dynamics of an
injected gas tracer (Han and Al-Dahhan, 2006).
Task 5: CFD studies
5.1 Evaluate and validate CFD models (two fluid model (TFM)) (using Fluent and/or CFX (Du et al.,
2006) and the discrete particle model (DPM) (Link, 2006; Link et al., 2004), or as it is also called, the
discrete element method (DEM) (Limtrakul et al., 2004)). Validate the available closures (including
particle-particle interaction forces, thermal expansion of the gas phase, by incorporating the energy
balance equation into the model (Antal et al., 2004)) against the detailed hydrodynamic parameters
obtained as benchmark data in Tasks 2, 3 and 4.
5.2 Use the validated CFD model(s) and closures to assess the proposed scale-up methodology for
hydrodynamic similarity with the change of conditions and scales. This success will allow for the use
of CFD models to look for the conditions and scales that ensure detailed hydrodynamic similarity by
searching different conditions to maintain similarity in the proposed scale-up parameters (solids
holdup distribution or radial profile, dimensionless spout diameter, and dimensionless fountain
height). Compare the findings with those obtained with dimensionless groups as scaling parameters
as evaluated in Tasks 3.2 and 3.4
5.3 Implement the validated CFD by studying the effects of other design and operating conditions on the
coater hydrodynamics that are not studied experimentally (e.g., dimensions, configurations, range of
flow rates, temperature (for the effect of thermal gas expansion), solids loading, solids type and size,
etc.). Assess the possible impact of the studied conditions on the performance of the coater in the
light of the hydrodynamics obtained using the validated CFD and closures.
Task 6: Simulating real TRISO fuel particles coaters using CFD
Use the validated CFD model(s) and closures to simulate the hydrodynamic parameters (listed in
6

<!-- page 7 -->

Tasks 2, 3 and 4) of the real TRISO fuel particle coaters at their operating conditions as reported in the
open literature and reports. Examine the effects of design and operating conditions (including thermal
expansion of the gas phase) on the kernels’ hydrodynamics and assess their impact on the performance of
the fuel particle coating processes (for example, as mentioned earlier, it was found that “flat” slug
formation above the gas inlet impaired bed circulation, which results in high numbers of non-spherical
particles, coating flaws, and missing coating layers).
Task 7: Identifying mimic conditions of the TRISO fuel particles coaters
Implement the validated CFD model(s) and closures, and if possible, the identified scaling
parameters (Tasks 3.2 and 3.4) to search for cold flow conditions (design and operating conditions,
including particle types and sizes, as outlined in Task 5.2) that can provide hydrodynamics similarity to
and mimic the real AGR TRISO fuel particle coaters, based on the validated proposed methodology for
scale-up and hydrodynamics similarity.
Task 8: Investigating the mimic TRISO fuel particles coaters using CT, CARPT, NGD, pressure
drop, optical probe, gas dynamic, and CFD techniques
8.1 Investigate the detailed hydrodynamics listed in and according to Tasks 2, 3 and 4 of the identified
cold flow conditions that mimic the AGR real 4-stage coated kernels (defined in Task 7) using the
advanced measurement techniques developed in Task 1 and those listed in Tasks 2, 3 and 4. Assess
the hydrodynamics obtained for the proper performance of the coater as mentioned earlier in Task 6.
8.2 Use the validated CFD models and closures to simulate these mimic conditions (Task 7) and compare
the simulation results with those obtained experimentally (Task 8.1) and with the simulation results
obtained for the real conditions of the 4 stage TRISO fuel particle coater (Task 6).
8.3 Assess and discuss the findings and draw conclusions.
Task 9: Write and submit the final report

Proposed Tasks
The following is an outline of the tasks proposed to accomplish the overall objectives set for the project:
Task 1: Development of on-line and non-invasive technique for process monitoring and
measurements
Develop a technique based on gamma ray densitometry that can be used on-line (on real TRISO coating
processes) to identify the spouting flow regime and to measure the radial profiles of solids and voidage
holdup, dimensionless spout diameter, and the dimensionless fountain height along the dimensionless bed
height in spouted beds. Develop flow regime identifiers for all spouted fluidized bed flow regimes, based
on signal processing of the measurements from the developed gamma ray densitometry technique. The
developed technique will be validated and tested against gamma ray CT. Implementing such a successful
development on commercial TRISO fuel particle coaters can be achieved by utilizing commercially
available NGD.
Work Accomplished:
Gamma ray densitometry (GRD) is a non-invasive radio-isotope based technique used to extract
useful information from multiphase systems. GRD consists of a sealed source (Cesium 137 of 250 mCi)
in a source holder and a NaI scintillation detector in front of the source. The source holder is mounted on
one side of a column, with the detector on the opposite side. A focused beam of radiation is transmitted
from the source, through the column and process material, to the detector. As the density of the material
in the column changes, the amount of radiation reaching the detector changes accordingly. It is generally
believed that the amount of radiation that reaches the detector through the process material is reflective of
its flow behavior and properties. Figure 1 shows the GRD with a source and a detector in front of it. The
beam of γ-rays coming from the sealed source is made such that it provides a point beam, which was
custom made for the requirements of measurement by Tracer Co Company.
GRD is used extensively in industry for such applications as level control, density measurement,
interface identification, and weight measurements in conveyors. It is widely used in the following
industries: chemicals, petrochemicals, off shore oil and gas, pharmaceuticals, cement, quarrying, solids
handling, paper and food. The major advantages of GRD that make it attractive in everyday industrial use
are,
1. Totally non-contact: Because the sources and detectors are mounted externally from the
column or process, they are completely unaffected by the conditions inside, however
extreme, providing reliable solutions when other technologies fail. They can be easily
accessed, installed or removed without the process being affected or interrupted.
2. High integrity: A non-invasive system mounted outside the vessel means no exposure or
wear by corrosive or abrasive products, and no need for construction to resist high
pressure, high temperature process conditions. This means less risk of leaks or emissions,
protecting processes, people and the environment.
3. High reliability and low maintenance: GRD measurements offer reliability and long
term performance. In addition, source checking is routine, simple and can be planned
well in advance.
4. Low installation costs: GRD can often be installed and commissioned without process
shutdown. Also, on most applications, no alterations to the reactors/columns are needed,
which means no expensive design changes for such implementation of GRD.
The radial profile of solids hold-up can be obtained with the help of GRD, which will determine
the performance of the reactor. GRD (Figure 2) technique is made to be flexible in order to accommodate
reactors up to 1.0 m in diameter. The entire set-up is constructed on wheels, which facilitates in the 3600
movement of GRD. It can also be moved in horizontal and vertical positions with the help of stepper
motors. Thus, measurements can be made using GRD along the diameter and height of the system under
study to obtain line averaged profiles.

Figure 2. Cross sectional view of 0.152 m spouted bed used for measurement in GRD system
GRD Electronics and Data Acquisition System. The traditional electronics system consists of
detector, timing filter amplifier, cables, high voltage power supply and BIN power supply. The electronic
system of GRD consists of NaI scintillating detector and Osprey USB interface. Osprey USB interface
consists of all the above components in a small tube shown in Figure 1, thus simplifying the electronic
system.
Most detectors can be represented as a capacitor into which a charge is deposited (Figure 3). By
applying detector bias, an electric field is created which causes the charge carriers to migrate and be
collected. During the charge collection small current flows, and the voltage drop across the bias resistor is
the pulse voltage. The preamplifier is isolated from the high voltage by a capacitor. The rise time of the
preamplifier’s output pulse is related to the collection time of the charge, while the decay time of the
preamplifier’s output pulse is the Resistor-Capacitor (RC) time constant characteristic of the preamplifier
itself. Charge-sensitive preamplifiers are commonly used for most solid state detectors. In charge-
sensitive preamplifiers, an output voltage pulse is produced that is proportional to the input charge. The
output voltage is essentially independent of detector capacitance. However, noise is also affected by the
capacitance. Additionally, the preamplifier also serves to provide a match between the high impedance of
the detector and the low impedance of coaxial cables to the amplifier, which may be located at great
distances from the preamplifier. The amplifier serves to shape the pulse as well as further amplify it. The
long delay time of the preamplifier pulse may not be returned to zero voltage before another pulse occurs,
so it is important to shorten it and only preserve the detector information in the pulse rise time. However,
most data consists of a range of pulse heights of which only a small portion is of interest.
10

<!-- page 11 -->

Figure 3. Detector and pre-amplification system
One can employ a multichannel analyzer (MCA, in GRD Osprey unit acts as an MCA) which
basically consists of an analog-to-digital converter (ADC), control logic, memory and display. The
multichannel analyzer collects pulses in all voltage ranges at once and displays this information in real
time. An input energy pulse is checked to see if it is within the selected range, and then passed to the
ADC. The ADC converts the pulse to a number proportional to the energy of the event. This number is
taken to be the address of a memory location, and one count is added to the contents of that memory
location. After collecting data for some period of time, the memory contains a list of numbers
corresponding to the number of pulses at each discrete voltage. The memory is accessed by a host
computer which is responsible for spectrum display and analysis as well as control of the MCA. The
block diagram for an MCA is shown in Figure 4.
Figure 4. Multichannel analyzer components with analog signal processing

The need for a single-input Pulse Height Analysis system for use with a Sodium Iodide detector is
served most simply by a photomultiplier tube (PMT) base MCA. The MCA includes a high voltage power
supply, preamplifier, amplifier, spectrum stabilizer and ADC in addition to its MCA functions, and thus,
there is no need for any NIM modules or a NIM Bin (conventional electronic system). All of this
capability is provided in the Osprey unit whose enclosure is no larger than a standard tube base
preamplifier, and the computer interface is via a USB port on the host computer or a USB hub (Figure 1).
Genie Basic Spectroscopy 2000© software is used to analyze the counts received from the detector. Using
these γ-ray counts, which is basically a time series is analyzed to obtain meaningful results. The time-
averaged cross sectional (along the diameter) data of the underlying detailed hydrodynamics is processed
statistically for determining the flow regimes.

Validation of GRD Results with Computed Tomography (CT) Results. The Dual Source
Computed Tomography (DE-DSCT) has been used as a CT. The DE-DSCT has two sources with 2 sets
of detectors (each set carrying 15 detectors) on the opposite of each source. The CT unit (Figure 7) is a
research setup designed to quantitatively determine the time averaged cross-sectional phase holdup
distribution of the phases in two phase systems or other multiphase systems. It has been designed to use a
sealed point gamma ray source 137Cs (~200 mCi or less). The source is placed in a source collimator
device (SCD) (Figure 9) which is made of lead. A fan beam arrangement of source-detectors (Figure 8) is
used for measuring the transmission data of the gamma ray photons across the multiphase experimental
setup. The fan beam consists of a longitudinal section of a cone. The point gamma ray source (here after
called source) is placed at the vertex of the cone. The detector array is placed along the bottom section of
the cone. The multiphase experimental setup is placed in the middle. The source is housed in a Source
Collimator Device (SCD) and is positioned such that it is at the geometrical center of the SCD to provide
maximum shielding.
The detectors count the gamma ray photons that survive and pass through the multiphase
experimental setup. They are capable of counting the unattenuated photons and the scattered photons.
Typically a threshold of energy is set and the photons that have energy above the threshold values are
counted. The image re-construction carried out later is based on this data.

Image Reconstruction Process. Expectation maximization (EM) (Lange, K. and Carson R.
1984) and alternating minimization (AM) (O’Sullivan J. A. and Benac J. 2007) algorithms have been
used to process gamma ray computed tomography data to obtain holdup distributions in various
multiphase systems. Both algorithms account for the stochastic nature of the gamma ray transmission
across the domain of interest. This makes these algorithms more favorable for image reconstruction to
determine the phase holdup distribution. Details of principles and discussions of EM and AM algorithms
can be found at (Kumar, S. B., 1994,Roy et al. ,2004,Varma et al.,2007).
The reconstruction of the attenuation coefficient image from the raw scanned data is obtained in a
stepwise process. It involves:
a. Averaging the raw data (average the data points of each projection).
b. Calculating the transmission ratios (the averaged data file for solids phase, along with the data
files for collected counts in air are used to calculate the transmission ratio (I/I ) of the scanned
o
section).
c. Calculating the length of the chords for each projection (based on the geometry of CT scanner and
the dimension of the scanned section).
d. Assigning initial guess (generated by assigning the linear attenuation coefficient of 0.08 cm-1 to all
the pixels in the square matrix which falls within or on the boundary of the test section).
e. Image Reconstruction (obtaining attenuation coefficients values on the domain).
f. Calculating the phase holdups (profiles of the solid and gas holdups distribution).
g. Averaging and plotting data (the time averaged cross-sectional solid and gas holdups profiles).
In each of the above steps, a corresponding code is used for data processing.
The γ-ray CT scanner in Missouri S&T has already been described in detail in 14th, 15th and 16th
quarterly reports. The sampling frequency for data acquisition to obtain adequate photon counts was
adjusted to 10 Hz. The number of samples for each projection was set to 60 which is enough to average
counts received by each detector for each projection. The solids used in this study were glass beads with a
mean diameter of 2 mm and a density of 2.55 g/cc. CT scans have been performed at six different levels:
3 inch, 5.5 inch, 7 inch, 11.5 inch, 13.5 inch and 15 inch from the distributor. For convenience, CT scan
results of 11.5 inch have been reported in this report and the rest will be reported in the coming quarterly
reports.
It is noteworthy that γ-ray CT experiments require intensive effort in both experimental work and in data
processing. In addition, careful arrangement and preparation need to be made to ensure the desired
accuracy. The image reconstruction using AM algorithm has been used to calculate the attenuation for
each pixel in the domain. The image reconstruction results have been shown in Figure 10. In the
following section we will describe the calculation steps necessary to obtain void and solids distribution in
the 2-D scan of the cross-section.

Flow Regime/Pattern Identification. The spouted bed was initially developed by Mathur and
Gishler (1974) as a means for drying wheat. Over the years, spouted bed has been used as an alternative
to fluidized bed for dealing with coarse particles, since spouted beds have large advantages over the
conventional fluidized beds. Spouted beds are mainly used for physical process like coating, granulation,
drying etc., but recently they have been used in chemical processes as well such as coal gasification and
catalytic reactions (Lopez et al., 2009). For a combination of bed geometry, solids phase and gas phase,
spouting can occur over a certain range of gas velocities. Three main flow regimes are observed in
spouted bed with increasing gas velocity (Epstein and Grace, 2011). They are packed bed, stable spouting
regime and unstable spouting regime. Bubbling and slugging flow regimes have also been reported in the
literature. Characterization of stable spouting is done by the formation of the stable spout/fountain.
Unstable spouting is usually where it is observed with swirling and pulsating of the spout with time.
Identification and prediction of such flow regimes is very important in the commercial application of
spouted bed.
Several important parameters have been studied such as, minimum spouting velocity; spout
diameter, fountain height and maximum spoutable bed height. But most of these parameters were
evaluated based on visual observation or through recording of a high speed camera in a transparent
spouted bed. Recently, the use of optical fiber probes, pressure transducer to analyze pressure fluctuations
has been employed to study the flow regimes in spouted bed (Wei et al., 2004; Wang et al., 2011). Optical
fiber probes is an invasive technique and pressure transducers measure the fluctuations at the wall and
cannot capture complete phenomena occurring in the reactor. Such evaluation of parameters is open to
objection due to accuracy of the results. Industrial scale reactors cannot rely on techniques which deploy
visual observation, techniques disrupting the flow dynamics and measurements taken at the wall, as they
are opaque and operate at high temperature and pressures. Hence, there is a need to evaluate techniques
involving non-invasive approaches. For lab scale and industrial scale reactors, there is a need to develop a
technique for flow regime diagnosis that is non-invasive, that can be easily implemented on an industrial
scale without disturbing the operation, and that provides reliable and accurate information. The primary
objective of this section is to demonstrate GRD non-invasive technique for online flow pattern/regime
demarcation.
Two spouted beds of 0.152 m and 0.076 m ID were used in the present study to identify the
different flow regimes (the detailed dimensions and geometry of spouted bed used are discussed in 3d
quarterly report). The spouted bed columns are constructed from plexiglass. The dimensions of the
spouted bed were made to satisfy the three conditions necessary for stable spouting to be achieved, which
are listed below.
D/d < 25~30 (18)
i p
D /D > 3~12 (19)
c i
H < H (20)
m
Compressed air was used as the gas phase, which was supplied by industrial scale high capacity
air compressor. Solids phase was glass beads of size 1mm, with a density of 2450 Kg/m3. In the present
study the time series signals of photon counts measured by the gamma ray densitometry in the gas-solid
spouted bed are analyzed statistically to identify the flow regime. The following statistical parameters are
estimated
1. Mean and variance
The mean, µ is calculated by:
N
µ=(∑Xi)/N (21)
i=1
Where, Xi is the collected counts at each sampling period and N are the total number of sampling
points. The absolute average deviation of the measured data from the mean can be obtained by:
€

(22)

1 N
σ2 = ∑(Xi−µ)2 (23)

Where N-1 is the number of points – degree of freedom.
2. Deviation from Poisson distribution:
The time series of gamma ray photon counts through a medium follows Poisson distribution in a
homogenous medium (air-liquid system, Shaikh PhD thesis) which states that the mean is equal to the
variance. Hence the ratio of the variance to the mean of the time series following poisson distribution is
unity. Such ratio has been used as a possible indicator to identify flow regime in bubble column (Shaikh,
2007). If the system deviates from the Poisson distribution the value of such ratio increases. Hence, the
indicator, I which is called flow regime indicator (Equation 10) as the ratio of the variance and mean.
Flow Regime Indicator (I) = (Variance)i/Mean (24)
Gamma ray densitometry experiments performed on bubble columns (gas-liquid system) by
Shaikh, 2007, used water as the liquid medium which is a homogenous phase before entering the gas.
Therefore, the photon counts for such a homogenous phase showed mean was close to variance. But for
the packed bed of solids before entering the gas to initiate the spouting, consists of solids and voids.
Hence, packed bed before spouting represents a heterogeneous medium which would not show mean
close to variance of the time series of gamma ray counts received by the detector and hence, it deviates
from poisson distribution. Such deviation would change in magnitude when the spouting is initiated and
flow regime changes. Hence, the analysis of regime identification of spouted bed (heterogeneous
medium) depends on the percentage of deviation from the counts received from packed bed.
Figure 11 and 12 shows typical photon counts received by NaI scintillation detector at minimum
spouting velocity and at stable spouting regime respectively, for over 30 seconds of data acquisition. The
flow regime transitions were identified using mean, variance and the ratio, I (percentage of deviation from
packed bed), as the indicators. The main criterion for differentiating the different flow regimes were
based on the difference between the successive values of the above mentioned three parameters.
When the spouted bed was operating in the packed bed regime, there was no change in the values
of mean, variance and the ratio (I). As the regime shifted from packed bed to stable spouting the values of
all the three parameters increased, thus indicating the capture of flow regime transition (Figures 13 – 18).
The same was observed when the flow regime shifted from stable spouting to unstable spouting regime.
The onset of stable spouting regime was found to be at 0.72 m/s and 0.58 m/s for 0.152 m and 0.076 m ID
spouted beds respectively. These transition velocities were in good agreement with the experimental
results obtained by pressure fluctuation measurements (3.4% overall difference) and with the comparison
of literature correlations (7.8% overall difference). The transition velocity for stable spouting to unstable
spouting regime was found to be at 0.79 m/s and 0.66 m/s for 0.152 m and 0.076 m ID spouted beds
respectively. The close agreement in the transition velocities for the flow regimes indicates the successful
demonstration of GRD technique as a means to identify different flow regimes in gas-solid systems. The
results obtained from the pressure transducers have been discussed in later sections of the report.

Task 2: CT, pressure drop fluctuation, and optical probe measurements and investigations
Identify two sets of particles and conditions according to the work of He et al. (1997) that have been
experimentally demonstrated to provide match and mismatch in radial solids holdup profiles and fountain
dimensionless height. Use CT and gamma ray densitometry (or NGD) to evaluate the selected conditions
from He et al. (1997), and adjust them as necessary to ensure the match and mismatch in these
parameters.
For the selected two sets of particles, investigate the effects of scale (coater size), design, and operating
conditions outlined earlier on the parameters listed below using CT, NGD, optical probes, and pressure
transducers along the bed for pressure drop and absolute pressure fluctuation measurements:
- Cross-section distribution and radial profile of the solids and voidage holdup along the bed height
- Dimensionless spout diameter along the bed height
- Dimensionless fountain height and ratio of the average fountain apogee to the bed diameter
- Ratio of the average fountain diameter to the bed surface diameter
- Average amplitude of slugs, measured as vertical displacement of the bed over the gas inlet relative
to the static bed height
- Ratio of the maximum apogee to the average apogee of fountain
- Longitudinal pressure profiles
The above mentioned time-series analyses (in Task 1) will be used to quantify the measurements of
these techniques at various conditions.
Work Accomplished:
He et al. (1997) proposed the following controlling non-dimensional parameters for scaling of
gd ρd u ρ H D
spouted beds: p , s p , f , , c , sphericity of particles (ϕ), interfacial angle of particle
s
u2 µ ρ d d
s p p
(φ), loose packed voidage (ε ), dimensionless particle size distribution and dimensionless bed geometry.
0
Using different spouted bed conditions that cover ambient and high temperature and pressure conditions
and different particle types and sizes, He et al. (1997) demonstrated that close hydrodynamic similarity
was maintained in the beds when these scaling parameters were closely matched. It is noteworthy that the
hydrodynamic similarity was demonstrated in these beds based on measurements of global parameters
such as dimensionless spout diameters, dimensionless fountain height, and pressure along the bed height.
The measurements were made using a video camera (which can see the region close to the wall), a ruler
attached to the column or to the window, and pressure transducers. Another point to note was that all
these experiments were performed in a semi-cylindrical column. The present work addresses the
limitations posed by the current dimensionless groups approach by measuring local parameters using the
developed optical probe and proposes a new mechanistic scale-up approach for hydrodynamic similarity.
Gas-Solids Optical Fiber Probes. Advanced gas-solid fiber optic probes are used for
measurements of local hydrodynamic parameters such as solids holdup, solids velocity and their time
series fluctuations. As discussed in previous quarterly reports (8th, 9th, 10th, and 11th quarterly reports),
advanced optical probes (Figure 19), which was acquired from Institute of Process Engineering of the
Chinese Academy of Sciences, works on the principle of back reflection of light was used in the present
study. The probes are capable of measuring solids holdup, solids velocity and their fluctuations all at the
same time. The details of optical probe measurement and analysis can be found in 8th, 9th, 10th, and 11th
quarterly reports.

(a) (b)
Figure 19. (a) Fiber optic probe (PV6) used in the present work; (b) PV6 being used in 0.076 m ID
spouted bed.
Pressure Transducers. A pressure transducer/sensor measures pressure, typically of gases,
liquids or solids. Pressure is an expression of the force required to stop a fluid from expanding, and is
usually stated in terms of force per unit area. A pressure transducer generates an electrical signal as a
function of the pressure imposed. The pressure transducer used for the present study measures gauge
pressure and is of the Model. No. PX309-002G5V purchased from Omega Dyne Inc. The pressure
transducer used is a single ended pressure measurement device. The data acquisition for the pressure
transducer consists of an A/D converter, which converts the pressure fluctuations into electrical signals.
Figure 20. Pressure transducer applied for measurement on 0.152 m ID spouted bed
26

*[figure(s) on p.26 → figures/scale-up-of-triso-fuel-coaters-via-advanced-measurement-and-computational-techni-p26.png]*


<!-- page 27 -->

The time series signals obtained from the transducer are then analyzed to obtain important
information about the hydrodynamics of the multiphase systems under study. These help in understanding
the flow pattern and flow dynamics of multiphase systems. The growth/collapse of bubbles and
movement of particles in the bed cause pressure fluctuations. As a result these signals provide valuable
information required for assessing the scale-up methods. Pressure fluctuations were analyzed for certain
chosen cases proposed by He et al. (1997) and also for the proposed new mechanistic hydrodynamic
scale-up approach (discussed later in the section).
Assessment of Dimensionless Groups for Spouted Bed Scale-up (He et al., 1997).
He et al. (1997) designed certain matched and mismatched experiments to verify their scaling
parameters for spouted beds mentioned in Table 2. Among these cases, in Case A and B the
dimensionless groups were matched and designed to study the validity of the scaling relationships. From,
cases C to G, the dimensionless groups were mismatched to examine the influence of each dimensionless
group on similarity; one or more groups were purposely mismatched. Here, the word match refers to
maintaining the same value of dimensionless groups in the prototype spouted bed (Case A) and model
spouted bed (Case B). The word mismatch refers to the variation of the values of one or more
dimensionless groups from cases C to G compared the prototype case A.

In this work, local and global hydrodynamic parameters were studied for the cases A, B, C and D
mentioned in Table 2. Cases A and B were studied due to the matching dimensionless groups, as it
evaluated the validity of the scaling relationships. Cases C and D were selected to due to the mismatch in
dimensionless groups and also to study the influence of these groups on the scaling relationship. Cases E,
F and G, were not selected under the present study as it was reported to have vast difference in the global
parameters by He et al. (1997) and hence was selected not to be repeated again for studying local
parameters. However, Cases A, B, C, D and E have been evaluated using Computational fluid dynamics
(CFD), which has been reported in later sections of this report. Optical probes were used to measure
solids holdup, solids velocity, solids mass flux and to also identify spout diameters. Due to the large
difference in the presence of solids in the annulus and spout region, the optical probes were able to clearly
differentiate these regions due to the nature of signals obtained. Hence, optical probes were helpful in
identifying spout diameters to a good accuracy. Pressure profiles using pressure transducers,
dimensionless fountain heights and maximum spoutable bed heights for the above mentioned cases were
also evaluated. Time series of pressure signals were also analyzed for these cases to look into the nature
of these signals and extract any meaningful information. The different measurement levels studied is
shown in Figure 21. To assess the validity of scaling relationships using dimensionless groups, more
focus has been given to the matching conditions (Case A and B) in the present study.
Figure 21 Schematic representation of different axial measurement levels performed on spouted bed.
The analysis of the cases can be divided into 5 different groups and each one is discussed in detail
in sections below. Solids holdup, solids velocity, solids mass flux, pressure profiles and pressure signal
analysis, spout diameters, fountain heights and maximum spoutable bed heights were analyzed for the
cases mentioned above.
Solids Holdup/ Solids Volume Fraction. Solids holdup is an important parameter in evaluating
the hydrodynamics of spouted beds. The solids holdup varies from the center of the spouted bed (spout
region) to the wall. Gas enters the bottom of the spouted bed as a jet and penetrates the solids bed. As a
result the amount of solids near the inlet of the spouted bed is low. As the gas moves up the spouted bed,
more solids are picked up which are being fed to the spout region from the annulus.

         With the increase of height in the spouted bed, the solids in the spout region increases as the
velocity of the gas decreases due to the penetration of the thick bed of solids. The maximum variation of
solids holdup is in the spout region due to this effect. Figure 22 shows the radial profiles of the solids
holdup in a 0.152 m ID spouted bed for conditions listed for Case A in Table 2. In the annulus, the
particles move slowly downward and inwards as a loose packed bed. The maximum concentration of
solids is in the annulus region and this remains same throughout the height of the spouted bed. As the gas
moves up the spouted bed and reaches the bed surface, a fountain region is formed. The solids then fall
back onto the bed surface by gravity, thus feeding the annulus region with solids. This creates a
systematic cyclic movement of particles in the spouted beds. Figure 23 shows the solids holdup profile in
the fountain region.

<!-- page 30 -->

                                                                                                                                                                                                        	
  
	
  

         The movement of gas phase is also very important in the spouted bed. Since the hydrodynamics
of the spouted bed is dictated by the gas phase, the evaluation of gas holdup profiles was done for
different cases. The maximum concentration of gas phase is in the spout region and the annulus region
has the lowest concentration of gas phase in the spouted bed. Due to the movement of solids and gas
phase, the overall system becomes a composite of a centrally located gas phase (moving upward)
surrounded by a dense-phase moving packed bed (moving downward). This systematic cycle movement
of solid and gas leads to solids/gas contact for many applications. Figure 24 shows the radial profiles of
gas holdup in 0.152 m ID spouted bed at different measurement levels. Figure 25 shows the radial profile
of gas holdup in the fountain region.

         The validity of the current scaling relationships should be assessed, which is based on the
dimensionless groups approach. The important case which is used to test this is the Case A (reference
model) and Case B (prototype model) listed in Table 2. The two cases are called matching conditions,
since the values of the dimensionless groups is same in both the cases. The evaluation of solids holdup
profiles and gas holdup profiles for these two cases should provide useful information regarding the
validity of scaling relationships. The solids holdup and gas holdup profiles for Case A listed in Table 2

        It is observed that the holdup profiles in the two different size spouted beds differ. The deviation
in the holdup profiles is majorly in the spout region. The profiles are mostly the same or very close to
each other in the annulus region. This is because the annulus acts as a loose packed bed and the dense
concentration of solids is maximum in this region. The spout has varying degrees of solids concentration
along the height of the spouted bed. The dimensionless groups, which control the hydrodynamics of the
spouted bed, cannot control the same solid phase holdup in the spout region when compared between the
two spouted beds. The absolute percentage deviations for the compared three levels were estimated. The
comparison of the holdup profiles at H/D level 1.1 has an average percentage deviation of 26.5% between
the two spouted beds. At level H/D 1.8 the average percentage deviation is 15%. This difference in the
percentage deviations at different levels is because of the higher turbulence caused by the gas phase at the
lower level.

         The gas phase variation between the profiles is also observed when the comparison of different
size spouted beds is made. As the level increases, the comparison of profiles has less deviation because
the solids phase volume fraction increases. More solids cross into the spout region from the annulus at
higher levels. The gas holdup remains low and constant in the annulus region due to high volume of
solids.
         Fountain region is the third distinct region in the spouted bed, where the solids after reaching a
particular height fall back onto the bed surface due to gravity. When the profiles were compared in this
region, the average deviation was found to be 23.71% for the two spouted beds. The amount of solids
falling back onto the bed surface depends on the incoming gas phase. The gas holdup profiles for both the
spouted beds are shown in Figures 32 (b), 33 and 34. Deviations in both the spouted beds have been
observed at the three levels of comparison.

        The difference in the profiles can be attributed to the dimensionless groups, even though matched
completely in both the spouted beds, these groups many not completely account for the entire
hydrodynamics. Additional investigation and assessment is required to draw such a conclusion. Solids
velocity was hence selected to assess further the dimensionless groups approach.

         Solids Velocity. The solids velocity profile of both the beds was also evaluated using the match
conditions listed in Table 2, with the use of optical probe. The details of estimation of solids velocity
using optical probe are discussed in 11th quarterly report. The solids velocity profile for Case A (0.152 m)
at different measuring planes is shown in Figure 35. The solids velocity is high in the spout region as it is
being picked up by the gas phase. The maximum velocity can be found in the center of spout region and
the velocity reduces as it reaches the spout-annulus interface. The velocity in the annulus is very low and
is negative. The solids move downwards in the annulus as a loose packed bed, as a result the velocity
tends to be low.

         Since, the assessment of dimensionless groups is the main objective the two spouted beds should
be compared for the velocity parameter. It is obvious that the velocity profiles in the two spouted beds are
different due to the different velocity of gas used in the different size spouted beds. To have a common
basis for comparison, the radial profiles of both the spouted beds were divided by minimum spouting
velocity (Ums) in order to dimensionalize. Minimum spouting velocity refers to the velocity at which the
onset of spouting occurs and below this velocity there is no spouting in the bed. This was evaluated
experimentally in both the beds. The gas was introduced into the spouted bed with very small increments.
The velocity at which the spouting occurred was noted down. To confirm the identified Ums, it was
compared with the correlations available in literature (16th quarterly report explains the detailed
correlations used for the present study). Figures 39, 40 and 41 show the compared radial profiles of
velocity in both the beds.
         The comparison of the different size spouted beds for the dimensionlazed radial profiles of solids
velocity show that the profiles are not similar. At H/D level of 1.1, the profiles had an average deviation
of 31.03%. The variation is maximum in the center of the bed (spout region) and decreases it reaches the
spout annulus interface (Figure 39).

        Additional investigation and assessment is required to draw conclusions on the applicability of
the dimensionless groups for hydrodynamic similarity of spouted beds. Solids mass flux was hence
selected to assess further the dimensionless groups approach.


        Mass Flow of Solids. To obtain the solids mass flow in spouted bed, two different sets of
experimental data need to be available. One set of experiments is to obtain the cross sectional averaged
solids holdup, εs. The second set of experiments is to obtain the solids velocity measurements. Hence, by
representing both the solids velocity and holdup by the sum of deterministic and fluctuating components,
the averaged solids mass flow in the spouted bed can be defined as:
                                                                                                                 rs

                                                                                          Ws = 2πρ s ∫ v s .ε s .r.dr                                                         (25)
                                                                                                                 0
                                                                                                                      R
                                                                                          Ws = −2πρ s ∫ v s .ε s .r.dr                                                        (26)
                                                                                                                      rs
         Where, 0 represents the center of the spouted bed, rs represents the spout annulus interface and R
represents the wall of spouted bed. Ws represent the solids mass flow, vs represents the solids velocity, ρs
represents the density of the solids and r represents the radial distance. The solids mass flow for the two
spouted beds were evaluated (Figure 42, 43 and 44) and then compared to evaluate the closeness of radial
profiles.

         Pressure Fluctuation Analysis. The analysis of pressure signals is very important as it gives the
information about bed fluctuations. The analysis of pressure fluctuation measurements was done by
pressure transducers. The details of the pressure transducer used for the current study is explained in the
earlier sections. Pressure transducers were attached at different axial heights of the spouted beds for
measurement and to check the effect of axial height on the measurement of pressure fluctuations. It was
found that the axial height did not have any drastic effect on identifying the flow regimes in the spouted
beds. But the fluctuations in the conical region of the spouted bed were more magnified and the intensity
of fluctuations decreased as the axial height was increased. This can be attributed the formation of
internal spout in the conical section of the spouted bed. Comparison of the standard deviations at different
axial heights showed that the standard deviation signals at conical region were larger than those measured
at different heights. This can be attributed to the turbulent motion of the gas near the spouted bed inlet. In
the present study, the pressure transducers were attached at H/D levels of 1.1, 1.8 and 2.5 for both the
spouted beds. Mean and variance were also estimated in order to give a further estimate of the signals.
The signals from 0.152 m and 0.076 m spouted bed were compared at H/D 1.1(Figure 48).
         The mean and variance for 0.152 m spouted bed were 0.4276 and 0.039677 respectively. The
mean and variance for 0.076 m spouted bed at H/D 1.1 were 0.126 and 0.013309 respectively. The
comparison shows that the fluctuations in the two beds are different and the hydrodynamics differ greatly.
The fluctuations analyzed for the levels H/D 1.8 and 2.5 are shown in Figures 49 and 50 respectively.

<!-- page 44 -->

The analysis of mean and variance for the two spouted beds at H/D levels 1.8 and 2.5 were
performed. At H/D 1.8 the mean for 0.152 m and 0.076 m spouted beds were 0.4181 and 0.1187
respectively. The variance was 0.040076 in 0.152 m spouted bed and 0.014236 in 0.076 m spouted bed.
The comparison between the two beds at this level also showed some differences. The nature of the
signals also explains the behavior of the bed. The non-similarity in the magnitude and frequency of the
fluctuations in 0.076 m and 0.152 m spouted is obvious from the nature of the fluctuations. However, in
the fountain region (H/D level 2.5), the pressure fluctuations were pretty close. The mean for 0.152 m and
0.076 m spouted bed was 0.2686 and 0.2517 respectively. The variance for 0.152 m and 0.076 m spouted
bed was found to be 0.02934 and 0.02331 respectively. This is because the fountain region is made of
solids falling back onto the bed surface due to gravity and solids volume fraction in this region is pretty
close as observed earlier.
Spout Diameter, Fountain Height And Maximum Spoutable Bed Height. Spout diameters,
fountain heights and maximum spoutable bed heights are among the key global parameters which are
helpful in determining the hydrodynamic similarity of the different spouted beds. These are considered to
be the global parameters and the similarity of these parameters also marks significance in scale-up
methodology.
The spout diameters (D) were evaluated using optical probes. The probes work on back
s
reflection of light, and the reflection of light is dependent on the number of particles in front of the probe.
Since there is huge difference in the degree of solids in spout and annulus, distinct signals are obtained in
these zones. The probe is first placed in the center of the bed and slowly moved towards the wall. The
point where there is distinct change in the signal that point of marked and the distance is noted. Fountain
height (H ) is the maximum height of solids achieved in the spouted bed. The initial bed height is marked
F
and then when the spouted bed is operated under the conditions of the experimentation, the maximum
height reached by them is noted. Thus fountain height is evaluated. Maximum spoutable bed height (H )
m
refers to the maximum amount of solids that a bed can accommodate beyond which the spouting does not
occur. It can also be termed as the amount of solids the spouted bed can process at a time. To estimate
this, the initial bed height of spouted bed was increased very gradually in small increments until the
spouting in bed did not occur. The bed height at that point was noted to bed the maximum spoutable bed
height.
The spout diameters was evaluated for Cases A, B, C and D. To compare the above cases, the
spout diameters (D) were dimensionalzed by dividing them with column diameter (D ). Figure 51
s c
represents the spout diameters for different cases studied. For convenience, one half (D/2) of the
s
complete diameter of the spout is shown in Figure 51. The spout diameter as a function of dimensionless
height (z/H) is shown. Z refers to the height of the plane of actual measurement and H refers to the height
of the entire spouted bed. The spout diameter for Cases A and B were close, however for Cases C and D
were large from the reference case A. The average deviation for Cases A and B was found to be 0.77%
and for Cases C and D were 3.8% and 3.4% respectively. Table 3 gives the details of the parameters
evaluated.

        Same procedure to dimensionalize was followed for fountain height (HF), which was
dimensionalized by dividing with column diameter (Dc). The evaluated parameters were then compared
with the reference case A. The deviation for Case B was 15%, which is agreeable in engineering terms.
However, for Cases C and D the deviation was 46% and 70% respectively, which is too large. The
deviations in Cases C and D show that the purposeful mismatching of the dimensionless groups gives
large deviations in global parameters. Based on this the deviations in local parameters is an expected
entity.

Based on the analysis of the local and global parameters on the dimensionless group’s
methodology, it can be stated that the differences in the matching cases is large considering the
engineering standards. Based on the experimental analysis performed in the present work the following
limitations can be drawn:
1. Match (Case A and B) and mismatch (case C and D) were identified to study the
dimensionless groups approach in the present study. The focus was based on the
matching conditions due the wide use of the approach.
2. The local parameters (solids and gas holdup profiles, solids velocity, solids mass flows)
assessed for the dimensionless groups approach have shown large percentage of
deviations in the radial profiles.
3. Solids and gas holdup profiles showed deviations in radial profiles for match conditions
in the spout region and the same trend followed when solids velocity was evaluated.
4. The deviations were prominent in the spout region, as the spout region is predominantly
dominated by the gas phase. The inflow of solids into the spout region from the annulus
varies along the height of the spout, thus leading to variation. The radial profiles
compared in the annulus region had little or no deviations. Since annulus acts as a
downward moving loose packed bed, the observed behavior is expected.
5. The analysis of pressure fluctuation signals showed different patterns in the spouted beds
at the compared levels of measurements. The statistical analysis of mean and variance
also confirmed the deviations in the studied spouted beds.
6. Global parameters (spout diameters, fountain height and maximum spoutable bed height)
showed good agreement for the matching cases. The deviations observed in the beds
based on these parameters were in the mismatch conditions, which were used to study the
influence of the dimensionless groups on the scaling relationships.
It has been demonstrated experimentally, that there is non-similarity in local hydrodynamics for
spouted beds when all dimensionless parameters are matched. The assessment of the parameters suggests
that more dimensionless groups are needed to explain the complete hydrodynamics of the spouted bed
system. Therefore, the scale-up methodology of dimensional analysis for scale-up of spouted beds should
be modified to establish a reliable scale-up methodology, not only considering the similarity in global
hydrodynamics, but also considering the similarity in local hydrodynamics.
New Method for Scale-up of Spouted Beds
Spouted bed is a two-phase system consisting of gas and solids. The gas phase enters the spouted
bed from the bottom as a jet and penetrates the bed of solid particles. As the gas phase penetrates the
solids, the solids are also being carried along with the gas. The gas carrying the solids, reaches the top of
the bed surface forming a fountain at the top. The solids then fall back on to the bed surface due to
46

**Table (p.46) — auto-extracted, verify against PDF:**

| Case | A | B | C | D |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Bed Diameter, D (m) c | 0.152 | 0.076 | 0.076 | 0.076 |
| Maximum Spoutable bed height, H (m) m | 0.390 | 0.184 | 0.235 | 0.235 |
| H /D m c | 2.56 | 2.42 | 3.09 | 3.09 |
| Deviation (%) | - | 5.65 | 20.4 | 10.4 |


<!-- page 47 -->

gravity. This nature of spouted bed creates three different zones namely: spout, annulus and the fountain.
The gas phase dictates the flow dynamics of the spouted bed. Hence, if the radial profile or cross sectional
distribution of gas holdup is maintained the same, then the two spouted beds would be similar in the flow
dynamics.
We propose a new hypothesis:
“Radial profile or cross sectional distribution of gas holdup should be the same for two beds to be
hydrodynamically similar”.
If this hypothesis were true, then the first step would be to develop a simple correlation to capture
the radial profiles using the key controlling dimensionless groups. This work attempts to evaluate the
proposed hypothesis utilizing optical probes and pressure transducers, which is based on selecting
conditions that provides similar and non-similar radial profiles of gas holdups in spouted beds. This
would be a difficult task as demonstrated earlier as the current dimensionless groups are not enough to
predict closely the radial profiles in two different spouted beds. The non-similar conditions are required to
demonstrate that even though the two spouted beds are geometrically similar and have similar
dimensionless groups, the radial profiles can be different leading to different flow dynamics in the
system.
Several trail experimentation was performed using optical probes to identify the conditions,
which would give similar and non-similar radial profiles in two spouted beds. Computational fluid
dynamics (CFD) was also used as another tool to help find these conditions (discussed in later sections of
the report). The experimental conditions were identified that gave similar and non-similar gas holdup
radial profile. These conditions were later simulated using CFD to compare with the experimental results
(discussed in later sections of the report).
The procedure for experimental evaluation of the proposed hypothesis is as follows:
1. Prototype/reference spouted bed was the 0.152 m (6 inch) spouted bed, which had the
same conditions of Case A listed in Table 1.
2. 0.076 m (3 inch) spouted bed was then used to perform experimental studies using optical
fiber probes and pressure transducers to identify the different conditions.
3. Once the two conditions were identified, a comprehensive evaluation (radial profiles of
gas and solids holdup, solids velocity, pressure fluctuations, spout diameters and fountain
heights) was performed on the identified conditions to validate the proposed hypothesis.
4. Perform computational fluid dynamics (CFD) simulations to further validate the
experimental results.
5. If the hypothesis is validated, then perform regression analysis for the identified similar
conditions to obtain a simple correlation based on important dimensionless groups which
control the complete flow dynamics of the two spouted beds.
6. If the hypothesis is validated, then this will motivate the development of Gamma ray
densitometry (GRD), a non-invasive radioisotope based technique, to be used for on-line
monitoring of the scale-up conditions identified and flow regime/pattern identification.
Table 5 shows the similarity and non-similarity conditions identified during this study. The
emphasis here is to show that if one maintains similar radial profiles of gas holdup, the flow dynamics of
the two systems will be the same. Such a similarity in the flow dynamics of the system is the ultimate
goal of any scale-up procedure to maintain the desired conversion and process performance.

        Since several parameters are evaluated for the similar and non-similar conditions identified in
Table 5, each of them is discussed in separate sections below.

         Gas holdup and solid holdup profiles. Gas holdup profiles evaluated for the two different size
spouted beds using similarity conditions are shown Figure 52. The measurement levels compared are at
H/D 1.1, 1.8 and 2.5 (fountain region). First the solids holdup was evaluated using optical fiber probes
and then the gas holdup was calculated (εg =1-εs). The comparison of radial profiles of gas holdup in both
0.152 m and 0.076 m spouted beds show the profiles were very close to each other. The absolute relative
difference between the two radial profiles was 4.1%, which is considered to be good in engineering terms.
This represents a good estimation of the newly utilized conditions which are successfully able to match
the profiles. In the scale-up approach of He et al. (1997), the voidage in the annulus region was almost
same, but differed drastically in the spout and in the founatin regions. Using the conditions, the profiles
in the spout and annulus region were matched after numerous trials.

        Solid holdup profiles for the similarity conditions are shown in Figure 54. The radial profiles

         Solids velocity. The solids velocity profile of both the beds was also evaluated for the similarity
and non-similarity conditions listed in Table 5, with the use of optical probe. The details of estimation of
solids velocity using optical probe can be found in 12th quarterly report. The solids velocity is high in the
spout region as it is being picked up by the gas phase. The maximum velocity can be found in the center
of spout region and the velocity reduces as it reaches the spout-annulus interface. The velocity in the
annulus is very low and is negative. The solids move downwards in the annulus as a loose packed bed, as
a result the velocity tends to be low. The particle velocity first increases near the inlet and then decreases
as the height of the spouted bed increases. In the fountain region, the velocity of solids carried by the gas
fall back onto the bed surface. The center of the fountain region has the maximum velocity and as the
radial distance increases, the velocity of the solids decreases.
         To have a common basis for comparison, the radial profiles of both the spouted beds were
divided by minimum spouting velocity (Ums) in order to dimensionalize. Minimum spouting velocity
refers to the velocity at which the onset of spouting occurs and below this velocity there is no spouting in
the bed. This was evaluated experimentally in both the beds. The gas was introduced into the spouted bed
with very small increments. The velocity at which the spouting occurred was noted down. To confirm the
identified Ums, it was compared with the correlations available in literature. Figure 56 shows the
dimensionless particle velocity profiles for the two spouted beds at measurement levels of H/D 1.1, 1.8
and 2.5. The absolute relative difference between the radial profiles was found to be 3.5%. The closeness
in the profiles was seen in all the three measured levels. The dimensionless particle velocity profiles for
non-similarity conditions were evaluated and the results are shown in Figure 57. The absolute relative
difference in the profiles was found to be 47.9%. The observed deviations were in the spout region and
the deviations in the annulus region were found to be very low or negligible. The velocity in the annulus

is very low and is negative because solids move downwards in the annulus as a loose packed bed, as a

         Pressure fluctuations. The analysis of pressure fluctuation measurements was done by pressure
transducers. The details of the pressure transducer used for the current study is explained in the earlier
sections. In the present study, the pressure transducers were attached at H/D levels of 1.1, 1.8 and 2.5 for
both the spouted beds. Mean and variance were also estimated in order to give a further estimate of the
signals. The signals from 0.152 m and 0.076 m spouted bed were compared at H/D 1.1(Figure 58).
         The mean and variance for 0.152 m spouted bed were 0.4276 and 0.039677 respectively. The
mean and variance for 0.076 m spouted bed at H/D 1.1 were 0.3961 and 0.02909 respectively. The
comparison shows that the fluctuations in the two beds are similar and the flow dynamics are similar. The
fluctuations analyzed for the levels H/D 1.8 and 2.5 are shown in Figures 59 and 60 respectively.

         The analysis of mean and variance for the two spouted beds at H/D levels 1.8 and 2.5 were
performed. At H/D 1.8 the mean for 0.152 m and 0.076 m spouted beds were 0.4181 and 0.3887
respectively. The variance was 0.040076 in 0.152 m spouted bed and 0.03423 in 0.076 m spouted bed.
The comparison between the two beds at this level also showed closeness in pressure fluctuation profiles
using the similarity conditions. The nature of the signals also explains the behavior of the bed. The
similarity in the magnitude and frequency of the fluctuations in 0.076 m and 0.152 m spouted is obvious
from the nature of the fluctuations. In the fountain region (H/D level 2.5), the pressure fluctuations were
pretty close. The mean for 0.152 m and 0.076 m spouted bed was 0.2686 and 0.2577 respectively. The
variance for 0.152 m and 0.076 m spouted bed was found to be 0.02934 and 0.02531 respectively. This is
because the fountain region is made of solids falling back onto the bed surface due to gravity and solids
volume fraction in this region is pretty close. The analysis of non-similarity conditions had huge
difference in the fluctuations and accounted for an absolute relative difference of 29.1%.

                                                               	
                                               54

<!-- page 55 -->

Pressure profiles were evaluated for the two spouted beds for both the conditions. The pressure
transducers were attached to the spouted bed at different heights and the pressure measurement was
evaluated. The overall bed pressure drop was also estimated using the pressure transducer by taking
readings at the inlet and at the outlet of the spouted bed. In order to compare the profiles, they were
dimensionalized by dividing the pressure values at each measurement plane by the overall pressure drop
of the bed. Figure 61 shows the profile, which shows that the similarity conditions had a close profile to
the reference case and the absolute relative difference was 13.4%. For the non-similarity condition the
difference was 26.4%.

Spout diameter, fountain height and maximum spoutable bed height. Global parameters such
as dimensionless fountain height, dimensionless spout diameter, and maximum spoutable bed height was
determined for the similarity and non-similarity conditions. The spout diameters (D) were evaluated
s
using optical probes. The probes work on back reflection of light, and the reflection of light is dependent
on the number of particles in front of the probe. Since there is huge difference in the degree of solids in
spout and annulus, distinct signals are obtained in these zones. The probe is first placed in the center of
the bed and slowly moved towards the wall. The point where there is distinct change in the signal that
point of marked and the distance is noted. Fountain height (H ) is the maximum height of solids achieved
F
in the spouted bed. The initial bed height is marked and then when the spouted bed is operated under the
conditions of the experimentation, the maximum height reached by them is noted. Thus fountain height is
evaluated. Maximum spoutable bed height (H ) refers to the maximum amount of solids that a bed can
m
accommodate beyond which the spouting does not occur. It can also be termed as the amount of solids the
spouted bed can process at a time. To estimate this, the initial bed height of spouted bed was increased
very gradually in small increments until the spouting in bed did not occur. The bed height at that point
was noted to bed the maximum spoutable bed height.
All the above parameters were also in close agreement with each other for the similarity
condition. For the non-similarity condition the deviations were found to large. Table 6 gives the detail list
of the dimensionless parameters and their corresponding deviations with the reference case.

         The analysis of local and global parameters for the two spouted beds using the similarity and non-
similarity conditions has been reported. It is observed that the radial profiles of the gas holdup in the two
spouted beds were maintained similar using the newly identified similarity condition. The non-similarity
condition was evaluated to demonstrate that even though there is complete matching of dimensionless
groups and geometrical similarity in the spouted beds compared, the radial profiles of gas holdups could
not be maintained similar. The hypothesis “Radial profile or cross sectional distribution of gas holdup
should be the same for two beds to be hydrodynamically similar”, can be said to be true in the present
study. There is a need to come up with a simple correlation to describe the flow dynamics of the spouted

Regression Analysis
Regression analysis was performed to obtain a correlation to describe the flow dynamics of the
spouted beds based on the newly identified similarity conditions. The equations of motion of solids and
gas were dimensionalized to identify the dimensionless groups for the dimensionless groups scaling
approach. Both Glicksman (1984) and He et al. (1997) ignored the effects of pressure term. However, if
the gas velocity is high enough to influence appreciably the thermodynamic properties of the gas, then
errors will be induced into the systems. Hence, along with the current dimensionless groups available, the
dimensionless pressure term was included for the regression analysis. The identified groups are:

utilized in the regression analysis.

Computed Tomography (CT) Investigations. A complete set of CT experiments to obtain the
phase distribution in spouted beds has been initiated. The experiments planned consist of scanning the
cross-section of spouted bed during actual flow experiments as well as several reference scans (e.g. air
only (empty column), column with solid). The CT scan will be performed for 0.152 m (6-inch) and 0.076
m (3-inch) spouted beds at the same conditions and at the same height levels that were measured by using
the optical probe listed in Table 2 and Table 5. The scan was performed at 3 inch, 7 inch and 11 inch from
the distributor along the vertical axis of 6 inch spouted bed column. The CT was utilized to measure
solids holdup radial profiles. A dual energy dual source γ-ray tomography (DE-DSCT) system developed
by Dr. Muthanna-Al-Dahhan at Washington University and has been set-up at Missouri S&T. The CT
results will be used to validate the optical probe, pressure transducer (reported in earlier section) and CFD
results (discussed in later sections) and further investigate the mechanistic scale-up approach. Figure 63
below shows a simple schematic diagram for 6-inch spouted bed column showing CT measurement
height levels.
Figure 63. 0.152 m (6 inch) spouted bed installed and centered into the CT equipment.
Before starting the CT scan experiment, the following input variables have to be specified:
1) Sampling mode
2) Frequency of data acquisition (sampling rate)
3) Output file format
4) Threshold level
5) Number of data sets
58

*[figure(s) on p.58 → figures/scale-up-of-triso-fuel-coaters-via-advanced-measurement-and-computational-techni-p58.png]*


<!-- page 59 -->

Figure 64. Schematic for 6 inch spouted bed column and the CT scans planned at different heights.
For 6-inch spouted bed column, the CT scan is performed at data acquisition frequency of 20 Hz
(every 50 milli sec), although a different frequency can also be chosen. For the number of data sets, 114
data points will be collected for each projection. This means each data point is collected in duration of 20
Hz i.e. t=0.05 sec. Therefore, the 114 data points will be collected in duration of 114 × 0.05 sec = 5.7 sec.
The reconstruction of the attenuation coefficient image from the raw scanned data is obtained in a
stepwise process. It involves:
h. Averaging the raw data (average the data points of each projection).
i. Calculating the transmission ratios (the averaged data file for solids phase, along with the data
files for collected counts in air are used to calculate the transmission ratio (I/I ) of the scanned
o
section).
j. Calculating the length of the chords for each projection (based on the geometry of CT scanner and
the dimension of the scanned section).
k. Assigning initial guess (generated by assigning the linear attenuation coefficient of 0.08 cm-1 to all
the pixels in the square matrix which falls within or on the boundary of the test section).
l. Image Reconstruction (obtaining attenuation coefficients values on the domain).
m. Calculating the phase holdups (profiles of the solid and gas holdups distribution).
n. Averaging and plotting data (the time averaged cross-sectional solid and gas holdups profiles).
In each of the above steps, a corresponding code is used for data processing. Below is the
transmission ratio for the 6-inch spouted bed column Where I represents empty column and I represents
0
column solids (solid only). This represents the first step to conduct the entire experiment.

        The solid and gas holdup profiles measured using CT at level 1 (3 inch from the gas distributor) is
shown in Figure 66. The nature of the holdup profiles are similar to the profiles reported from the
evaluation of optical probes and CFD studies. The solids volume fraction at the center is low and then
increases until it reaches the spout-annulus interface. In the annulus region the solids volume fraction
remains constant. This is because the annulus acts as a loose-packed bed moving down. The maximum
concentration of solids is in this region. In the spout region, the solids are being picked up by the
incoming gas, as a jet, and hence the solids volume fraction here is less.
