solids holdup is in the spout region due to this effect. Figure 40 shows the radial profiles of the solids

*[figure(s) on p.61 → figures/scale-up-of-triso-fuel-coaters-via-advanced-measurement-and-computational-techni-p61.png]*


<!-- page 62 -->

holdup in a 6 inch spouted bed for conditions listed for reference case in Table 1. In the annulus, the
particles move slowly downward and inwards as a loose packed bed. The maximum concentration of
solids is in the annulus region and this remains same throughout the height of the spouted bed. As the gas
moves up the spouted bed and reaches the bed surface, a fountain region is formed. The solids then fall
back onto the bed surface by gravity, thus feeding the annulus region with solids. This creates a
systematic cyclic movement of particles in the spouted beds. The solids holdup increase in the spout
region as the height of the spouted bed increases. This trend is seen with the measurements reported by
optical probe and CFD and the CT scans reiterates the same trend.

Figure 69. Comparison of radial profiles of solids holdup using CT and optical probe at level 2 (7 inch
from the gas distributor).
Figure 69 shows the comparison of the solids holdup profile measured from the optical probe and
CT scans. Both the techniques report the same trend and are very close to each other with a deviation of
9.6%. This deviation in the measurements can be attributed to the intrusive nature of optical probes used
for measurement. CT being a non-invasive technique will be able to give a good estimate of the flow
dynamics. The assessment of the newly developed mechanistic scale-up method is being performed in the
current quarter to further validate the findings from optical probe experimentation and CFD simulation.
Activities in progress (expected to be completed by December 2012):
Assessment of the selected conditions of the new methodology for hydrodynamic similarity and to
evaluate following parameters:
• Radial profile of the solids and voidage holdup along the bed height.
• Dimensionless spout diameter along the bed height.
• Dimensionless fountain height and ratio of the average fountain apogee to the bed diameter.
• Ratio of the average fountain diameter to the bed surface diameter.

m
Maximum spoutable bed height is an important characteristic in the design, operation and scale-
up of spouted beds. It is directly related to the amount material that can be processed, beyond which the
spout in the bed terminates. The bed has to be loaded below this height in order to maintain stable
spouting conditions in the bed. In the present work, H was determined by adding particles into the bed
m
until spouting in the bed ceased for any gas velocity. This can also be termed as “spout termination”. The
present work focuses on the effect of particle size, gas velocity and gas inlet diameter on maximum
spoutable height. The experimental data was compared with correlations available in the literature.
Effect of particle size. Three different particles sizes of 1mm, 2mm and 3mm in diameter were
studied, which was made of glass beads and had a density of 2450 kg/m3. The experiments were
performed in 0.152 m and 0.076 m spouted beds. McNab and Bridgwater (1977) is the most commonly
used equation for predicting Hm in spouted beds. The current experimental data was compared with
equation 35.

The maximum spoutable bed height H with respect to particle size has been reported by several
m
investigations. Mathur and Epstein (1974) indicated that H increases with particle size (d ) up to a limit
m p
and then decreases, the critical particle size commonly ranges from 1.0 to 1.5mm for the columns in
different diameters. This tendency was demonstrated by later studies (Littman et al., 1977 and 1979 and
McNab et al., 1979). In another study, Rovero et al. (1983) indicated that H increased when the particle
m
diameter was increased from 0.3 to 0.45mm in an 80mm diameter flat-based column and then H
m
decreased when the particle diameter was increased from 0.65 to 1.24 mm. The results of Chandnani and

Epstein (1986) showed that Hm increased as the particles size was increased from 0.40 to 0.71 mm and
then decreased.
         This trend of the maximum spoutable bed height with respect to particle size can also be seen in
the previous reports by Grbavcic et al. (1991), Cecen (1994), Dogan et al. (2000) and Freitas et al. (2000).
The effect of particle size on the maximum spoutable bed height Hm of present spouted bed is presented
in Figure 70. The present experimental data showed that the effect of particle size on Hm of spouted bed
increases first and then decreases with increasing particle size. The critical particles size in the present
study was in the range of 1.0 to 2.0 mm.
         McNab & Bridgewater (1977) correlation is the most commonly used correlation and hence the
experimental data was compared to it and found the existence of large deviations (Figure 70). Comparison
with the Littman (1977) showed a completely different trend, as the correlation predicts the decrease of
Hm with increase of particle size, which was also reported by (Zhong et al., 2006). This trend was found
to be not true in the present case and also by comparing with many correlations available in the open
literature.
         Due to the presence of large deviations present in the experimental data of both the 0.152 m and
0.076 m spouted bed when compared to equation 35, the data was made to fit equation 35 with
appropriate changes in the correlation. As a result, the corrected correlation (Figure 71) is presented in
equation 39.

common trend reported in most of the open literature. The critical particle range for the current

decrease with increase in particle size.

         Effect of inlet diameter. In the present study three sizes of inlet diameter were studied (0.063 m,
0.0127 m and 0.0190 m). Figure 72 shows the effect of inlet diameter (Di) on the maximum spoutable bed
height (Hm) of present spouted bed and some spouted beds in the open literature. It can be seen that Hm
deceases with increasing inlet diameter without reference to the introduction of spouting gas. In the
literature, Littman et al. (1979) specially studied the effect of inlet diameter on Hm and demonstrated that
Hm deceased with increasing inlet diameter. Same tendency has been observed by later investigations
(e.g. Chandnani et al., 1986; Cecen et al., 1994 and Freitas et al., 2000). Dogan et al. (2000) indicated that
Hm increased from the 2mm inlet diameter width (Di/Dt = 0.013) to the 6mm inlet diameter width (Di/Dt
= 0.04) and then decreases with inlet diameter width, as presented in Figure 72. Nevertheless, Dogan et
al. (2004) in their later publication showed that Hm increased slightly with increasing inlet diameter size.
According to the above review, we can draw a generally conclusion that Hm of spouted beds might
decrease with increasing inlet diameter size. In the present work, it is found that the increase of inlet
diameter size decreases Hm. Even after many years of research, there still remains lot of ambiguity on the
effect of inlet diameter on Hm. This parameter needs to be studied more. Due to the shortage of time, the
present work was able to focus until this level and more detailed investigation is opened for future
research.

Effect of gas velocity. There has been many investigations on the maximum spoutable height H
m
of a spouted bed, especially the discussion on the effect of gas flow rate on Hm. Rao et al. (1985)
theoretically studied H of a spouted bed under the condition that normal spouting was accompanied by
m
additional aeration but no fluidization of the annulus. Their experiments showed that H decreases with
m
increasing gas flow rate. Grbavcic et al. (1991) studied H of a flat base spouted bed at minimum
m
spouting flow rate and obtained the same results. These studies were carried out at a narrow range of gas
flow rate (less than minimum spouting gas flow rate). However, previous work (Zhong et al., 2006 a, b
and c) illustrated that spouting could also be observed when the gas flow rate is larger than the minimum
spouting condition. Figure 73 presents the effect of gas flow rate on the maximum spoutable bed height.
In which, h is the ratio of maximum spoutable bed height with gas to without gas. It can be seen that the
maximum spoutable bed height decreases with gas flow rate. This appears a similar tendency to the
previous work by Rao et al. (1985) for 600 cone base half column and Grbavcic et al. (1991) for flat base
full column. This result indicates that the spoutable bed height decreases with gas flow rate without
reference to bed geometry and operating condition. This parameter needs to be studied more. Due to the
shortage of time, the present work was able to focus until this level and more detailed investigation is
opened for future research.

Effect of Operating Variables (Particle size, Gas inlet diameter, Particle density, Static bed
height and Gas velocity) on Spout Diameter. Numerous researchers have performed studies on
determining average spout diameter and also the influence of different solids and fluid properties on them.
Spout diameter is an important parameter in spouted beds as it dictates the hydrodynamics of the
complete reactor. Complete knowledge of this parameter is essential in modeling a design of spouted bed.
MacNab (1972), Bridgwater and Mathur (1972), Green and Bridgewater (1983) and Lim and Grace
(1987) have all reported correlations for predicting average spout diameter, but most of them are still
bound by large percentage of deviations. In the present work, factorial design experiments are proposed to
predict the average spout diameter. The motivation for the present work was the successful
implementation of factorial design in predicting average spout diameter in a two-dimensional spouted bed
by F. Zanoelo et al. (2004).

<!-- page 67 -->

In this work a factorial design of experiments is proposed in order to analyze the influences of
operating parameters on the mean spout diameter. The effects of solid density, static bed height, particle
diameter, superficial gas velocity and inlet diameter was studied. A set of experiments has been carried
out using 0.152 m conical based cylindrical spouted bed. The spouted bed consists of a transparent
column of cylindrical cross-section made of PVC. The bed has ten axial ports for the sake of
measurements. The air was provided by a high capacity industrial air compressor which has a capacity up
to 200 psi.
A 25 factorial design of experiments has employed in order to investigate the effect of operating
parameters. The experiments were run in a completely randomized design. This involved a total of 32
experimental measurements of mean spout diameter. Each factor had 2 levels of measurement (one high
and one low). Two replications were performed in order to estimate the experimental error. A total of 64
experimental measurements were performed.
The 2k design is particularly useful in the early stages of experimental work, when it is desired to
investigate the effects of a large number of variables (Montgomery, 2001). Glass beads and steel shots of
densities 2450 and 7400 kg/m3 respectively were used as solid particles. Two particle sizes of 1 mm and 2
mm in diameter were considered for the experiment. The gas velocity used was 0.74 m/s and 1.08 m/s.
The static bed height used in the experiments was 0.325 m and 0.390 m. The inlet diameter sizes used was
0.006 m and 0.012 m.
The five variables analyzed in this work were chosen based on the following criteria: the variable
appears in correlations found in the literature to calculate the average spout diameter of conventional
spouted beds and/or previous experimental observations indicate a possible significant effect of the
variable on the spout shape and width. At each operating condition, the spout width profile was obtained
by optical fiber probes. The optical probe works on the principle of back reflection of light. The spout and
annulus have completely different degrees of solids. Hence, the reflected light by the solid particles in
these two regions are completely different and thus helps in determining the spout diameter. SAS and
MINITAB software was used to perform the factorial analysis. The factorial design was performed in
coded units for simplification of usage in the software.
Factorial design. The experimental runs were coded for use of statistical software. The high level
was denoted as 1 and the lower level was denoted as -1. The factorial design was generated in MINITAB
software. The experimental runs were completely randomized using MINITAB. The factorial design is
shown in Table 7. Table 7 shows just one experimental run consisting of 32 experimental measurements
of spout diameter.

         A completely randomized design relies on randomization to control for the effects of any
extraneous variables. The two replications are done to estimate any experimental error. It is necessary to
remember that a statistical analysis for a 25 factorial design involves 5 main effects, 10 two-variable
interactions, 10 three-variable interactions, 5 four-variable interactions and 1 five-variable interactions.
The significance level in order to analyze the results was set 0.05.

         ANOVA table. Analyzing the factorial design in MINITAB software, the ANOVA table for the
experimentation was generated. Since the experimental measurement is replicated twice, ANOVA table
(Table 8) and the normal probability plot can be used to determine the significant effects. All the data
point fall around the mean value. Analyzing the ANOVA table, it was observed that all five main effects
(solids density, static bed height, particle diameter, superficial gas velocity and inlet diameter) were
significant. The two way interactions suggested that the interaction between particle size and inlet
diameter and gas velocity and inlet diameter were significant. The three-way, four-way and five-way
interactions were found to be not significant from the ANOVA table. The effects that are negligible are
normally distributed with mean equal to zero and tend to fall along a straight line, while the significant
effects have non-zero means and do not lie along the straight line (Montgomery, 2001). In the case of
Figure 74, it is quite evident that except for the two-variable interaction CE and DE and the main effects,
all of them are approximately equal to zero.

Task 3: CARPT Measurements and Investigations
For the identified sets of conditions that provide the best match and best mismatch of the
solids/voidage holdups, radial profiles, dimensionless spout diameters, and dimensionless fountain height,
investigate the detailed 3D solids flow field, velocity, and turbulent parameters using CARPT. The
following parameters will be measured and studied: 3D flow pattern, 3D velocity components, 3D
turbulent parameters (shear stresses, normal stresses, kinetic energy, granular temperature, eddy
diffusivity), particle-particle interaction, circulation time distribution, stagnant zones, local residence
time, solids flux mass balance (solids fluxes upward and downward), solids Lagrangian trajectories,
solids mass circulation rate, dimensionless measure of cycle time, and average amplitude of slugs
measured as the vertical displacement of the bed over the gas inlet relative to the static bed height
(Bhusarapu, 2005; Luo, 2005). In addition to these parameters, the following can be obtained from
CARPT as well, and will be compared with those obtained in Task 2: dimensionless fountain height,
dimensionless spout diameter, 3D distribution and radial profile of solids/voidage (Mabrouk et al., 2005),
ratio of the average fountain diameter to bed surface diameter, ratio of the average fountain height to the
bed diameter, ratio of the maximum apogee to the average apogee of fountain ejecta, and average
amplitude of slugs measured as the vertical displacement of the bed over the gas inlet relative to the static
bed height.
Work Accomplished:
A study involving implementation of radioactive particle tracking technique around the spouted
bed reactor experimental set-up is being planned at Missouri S&T. Such a study will provide valuable
information on solids flow in 3-D, solids velocity and its components, overall residence time distribution,
local residence time distribution, stagnant zones, solids occurrence, lagranagian trajectory and other
related solids flow dynamic parameters. This technique is based on following the motion of a radioactive
particle (also known as radioactive tracer) in 3-D domain using an array of scintillation detectors (Al-
Dahhan, 2009). This tracer will be dynamically similar to the tracked phase. In this study, a radioactive
tracer will be identical in size, density, and shape to that of the solids (glass beads). This tracer will be a
parylene coated particle made of irradiated Scandium-46. The parylene coating is necessary to increase
chemical resistance and mechanical strength of the Scandium-46 tracer particle. The radioactive tracer
will be allowed to move freely in the system and the operating conditions will be kept constant during an
experiment. The instantaneous tracer position will be then identified by simultaneous monitoring of
radiation intensities received by a set (usually 16-32 No.) of NaI detectors arranged strategically around
the system (Roy et al. 2002, Rammohan et al., 2001). In this study, 32 detectors will be arranged around
the spouted bed reactor experimental set-up. The previous design of multi-channel data acquisition system
(Devnathan N., 1991) is a combination of components from Nuclear Instrumentation Module (NIM) and
Computer Automated Measurement and Control (CAMAC) standards. Previous design uses GPIB based
crate controller (Kinetics Systems 3988-G3A) and List sequencing crate controller (3982-Z1B) to read
humongous multi-channel data from scalars, stores it temporarily in the buffer memory and transfers it to
PC in a systematic manner using General Purpose Interface Bus (GPIB). As these components have
become obsolete, it will be replaced with a CC-USB CAMAC crate controller from WIENER. Additional
effort has been spent on changing existing data acquisition programs to accommodate this major change.
This change is essential; as data transfer will be via Universal Serial Bus (USB)-2 interface which is
much faster than the existing GPIB. The detectors and components of associated electronic system have
been received in the present quarter.
The radiation intensity recorded at each detector decreases exponentially with increasing distance
between the tracer and the detector. The analytical solution to the forward problem of finding tracer
position based on counts received in the detector is not possible (Al-Dahhan M, 2009). This is carried out
by performing a set of calibration experiments which uses a calibration device and methodology at the
same operating conditions as that of RPT experiments. A radioactive tracer will be placed at various
known locations and radiation intensities received at the detectors will be recorded. Using this intensity
72

<!-- page 73 -->

data, calibration curves for each detector will be established. The calibration curve relates the intensity
received at a detector to the distance between the tracer and the detector. Position reconstruction
algorithms use this data from calibration curves to estimate the particle position at a given sampling
instant of time.
A calibration methodology involving a manual calibration device (Figure 76) suitable for spouted
bed application is designed, and manufactured. Basically, it consists of a rod which has a vial at tip
containing radioactive particle. With the help of guide bush arrangement the rod can slide in/out to place
particle at any height. The position can be recorded with the help of scale attached to the rod. This rod can
be placed at selected radial positions. Once particle is placed at known position, data acquisition program
will acquire multichannel counts data in calibration mode of program. This design allows having
calibration points in different regions of spouted bed which is necessary from RPT experiments point of
view. This calibration device is tested with naked cold particle and found to work satisfactorily.
(a) (b)
Figure 76.a.Detector arrangement schematics; b. Manual calibration device
The above mentioned calibration methodology is manual, invasive and time consuming which
could make it of limited use for this study of dense granular flow. Hence, the newly developed and
assembled automatic calibration apparatus (Figure 77) will be used for the current study. This provides an
edge over the traditional calibration procedure which (Figure 76) was manually placing the test tracer and
then recording the counts. The new calibration set-up offers the leverage of automation and faster (less
time consuming) calibration. It is currently being evaluated for its application in spouted bed. The
radioactive tracer particle to be used in the spouted bed study is being prepared in order to be used in the
dynamic experiments.

Figure 77. (a) RPT electronics cabinet; (b) New calibration apparatus and (c) Detector set-up for RPT
Tracer preparation. The RPT technique is based on following the motion of a single radioactive
particle (γ-emitter) in the 3-D domain of the whole system. Development of a radioactive tracer particle
mimicking glass beads used in this study, an important and challenging task, is pivotal to the success of
RPT technique results. This task involves selection of suitable radioisotope, activity selection, particle
size selection and fabrication, sealing inside vials, irradiation of sealed vials, preparation of tracer particle
inside hot glove box, density matching, sealing radioactive particle inside tracer particle and initial testing
of tracer particle for contamination. Feasibility of using Scandium-46 based tracer particles has been
checked against cobalt-60 and decided to use Scandium-46 based tracer particles due to its short half-life
(half-life ~84 days) compared to cobolt-60 (~5.2 years) and lesser irradiation time requirement. Use of
Scandium-46 is reasonable for spouted bed reactor applications as the movement of glass beads is very
fast and it will not require half- life correction. Scandium-46 based tracer particle preparation is in
progress which involves safe handling of radioactive particles inside glove box. A hot glove box suitable
to perform safe handling of radioactive particles has been prepared (Figure 78) and 4 number of dry runs
has been carried out on dummy vials containing scandium particles to demonstrate the newly developed
vial handling and opening procedure. Hot glove box houses optical Microscope with LCD screen,
shielding arrangement for safe cutting of irradiated vials and subsequent tracer preparation related
activities. Tracer preparation is the bottleneck activity in implementation of RPT around multiphase
system which involves lot of sub-activities and also involvement of number of internal and external
agencies such as Radiation safety, MURR etc. This activity has to be carried out as per Radiation
protocols and procedures approved by Missouri S&T EHS department. Currently, implementation of
these protocols and dry runs as per the approved procedures has been carried out. In the next few weeks,
main focus will be to prepare scandium-46 based tracer particle suitable for this study and initiate the
validation of new scale-up methodology using RPT technique.
74

*[figure(s) on p.74 → figures/scale-up-of-triso-fuel-coaters-via-advanced-measurement-and-computational-techni-p74.png]*


<!-- page 75 -->

Figure 78. RPT Glove box
Electronic system development and programming. The older design of RPT multi-channel
data acquisition system is a combination of components from Nuclear Instrumentation Module (NIM) and
Computer Automated Measurement and Control (CAMAC) standards. Previous electronic system design
uses GPIB based crate controller (Kinetics Systems 3988-G3A) and List sequencing crate controller
(3982-Z1B) to read humongous multi-channel data from scalars, stores it temporarily in the buffer
memory and transfers it to PC in a systematic manner using General Purpose Interface Bus (GPIB). As
these components have become obsolete, it forced a critical change to be made in the hardware
configuration of RPT. This demanded to find suitable controller and reprogramming without making
major replacements to already procured NIM and CAMAC based components. The newly developed GUI
(graphical user interface) based data acquisition program and controller (CC-USB crate controller from
Weiner Inc.) has been tested extensively using test sources. The schematics of RPT electronics along with
modifications is shown in the Figure 79.
The newly developed data acquisition system has three modes of operation: Track, Calibration,
MCA (Multichannel Analyzer). In Track mode, actual particle tracking experiments are performed. It
requires user to provide input of sampling frequency, total sampling time, and threshold settings on
discriminator. Calibration mode is used to perform RPT calibration by providing input of tracer particle
position and recording counts in each detector at user defined sampling frequency. In MCA mode, gamma
spectroscopy is carried out for each detector to find the position of photo peak in each detector channel.
Due to the hardware limitations, one value of threshold is set for all channels. This requires
synchronization of photo-peaks in all the channels. This is usually done by varying fine and coarse gain
on timing filter and amplifiers. Reprogramming to incorporate all the above mentioned modes and
associated troubleshooting consumed significant amount of time. The newly developed data acquisition
system is ready for actual experiments.
75

*[figure(s) on p.75 → figures/scale-up-of-triso-fuel-coaters-via-advanced-measurement-and-computational-techni-p75.png]*


<!-- page 76 -->

Figure 79. Modified RPT electronics (Red color indicates new components/standards/Programs)
Calibration RPT data acquisition and motion control electronic system. Calibration RPT set-
up requires scintillation detector based counting system and motion control components to track the
moving tracer particle. The schematic of proposed data acquisition and motion control electronic system
is shown in Figure 80. The scintillation detector (Canberra-NaI-802) along with photo-multiplier tube
(PMT) and pre-amplifier will be placed inside collimated shield driven by stepper motor. This new
collimated shield will cover only the crystal portion of the scintillation detector. The signal coming out of
pre-amplifier will be feed to timing filter and amplifier to shape the pulses. These shaped pulses will be
feed to multifunction device (NI USB-6221) which will measure the height of pulses in terms of voltage
and will feed to LabVIEW software. LabVIEW software will be the common software platform for
radiation detection system and motion control. The count rate information acquired in LabVIEW will be
feed to motion control sub-routine in LabVIEW which will control the motion of stepper motors driving
detector movement. Depending on count rate information, the motor will rotate in either direction to track
the moving tracer particle. In this manner, real time tracking of tracer position using collimated detectors
can be carried out. Hence obtained positions will be cross-correlated with the counts obtained in non-
collimated detectors surrounding set-up. In this manner, calibration map for the system can be generated
in a non-invasive manner while running the actual tracking experiments. This will be done dynamically
and feasibility of combining with Monte Carlo procedure based calibration map generation procedure will
be checked. In this proposed hybrid method of calibration map generation, handful calibration data points
obtained from dynamic calibration set-up will be used in the analytical model of RPT and using Monte
Carlo procedure calibration maps will be generated for thousands of calibration points in the system.
76

Task 4: Gas dynamic measurements and investigations
For the conditions studied in Tasks 2 and 3, characterize and investigate the gas dynamics and its
dispersion, using our newly developed technique based on the measurement of the dynamics of an
injected gas tracer (Han and Al-Dahhan, 2006).
Work Accomplished:
The gas residence time distribution is of considerable importance in predicting the conversion and
selectivity for various catalytic reactions, and backmixing is undesirable as it leads to increased by-
products.
Both vertical and horizontal mixing/dispersion can be studied using steady and unsteady state
tracer techniques. In the steady state tracer experiment, a steady flow of tracer gas is introduced into the
spouted bed at a certain location, and the tracer concentration is measured either downstream or upstream
of the injection point. Ideally, the injection rate should be adjusted to match the local gas velocity in the
bed to achieve an isokinetic injection (Bader et al., 1988). Based on the tracer concentration measured
upstream of the injection point, the axial backmixing coefficient can be derived (Kunii and Levenspiel,
1991). On the other hand, the radial dispersion coefficient is obtained by analyzing radial profiles of
tracer concentrations measured downstream of the injection point (Bader et al., 1988). The overall or
effective axial dispersion coefficient over the entire bed could be derived using the unsteady state tracer
technique.
For gas-solid multiphase systems, such as bubbling fluidized beds, circulating fluidized
beds/risers and downers, there have been a large number of researches on gas backmixing and/or radial
dispersion (e.g. Sotudeh-Gharebaagh and Chaouki, 2000; Sane et al., 1996; Cao and Weinstein, 2000; Bi,
2004; Bai et al., 1992; Wang and Wei, 1999), while there have been only a few studies on cylindrical
spouted beds (e.g. Sun et al., 2005; Lim and Mathur, 1974, 1976; San Jose et al., 1995; Olazar et al.,
1993d, 1995a; Wang et al., 2004). In the current study, CFD was used to evaluate the residence time
distribution in spouted bed, since CFD has already been validated to predict closely the hydrodynamics of
the spouted bed (discussed in later sections of the report). Helium was chosen as the tracer because it is
inert and non-adsorbing on glass beads. The tracer was simulated to be introduced as a step function, and
the unsteady state response was evaluated. The details of the CFD simulation for spouted bed
hydrodynamics has been reported in 16th, 17th and 18th quarterly reports.
The CFD simulation for estimating the residence time distribution was performed. The
cumulative distribution function obtained for the current simulation in shown in Figure 81. The RTD
function E(t) is estimated by using the equation 45.
E(t) = dF(t)/ dt (45)
The response time lag t is defined as the time difference between the start of the sampling and the
0
start of the response, and can be estimated easily from the RTD function E(t). Generally, knowing the
response time lag, the mean residence time tm can be computed. The equation to estimate mean residence
time is shown in equation 46.
∞ ∑(t−t ).E(t−t )
tˆ = ∫(t−t ).E(t−t ).d(t−t ) = 0 0 (46)
0 0 0 ∑E(t−t )
0 0
The mean residence time for the 6 inch spouted bed was found to be 0.87 seconds.
Activities in progress (Expected to be completed in December 2012)
• The effects of bed size, gas velocity and solids on the back-mixing of the gas phase will be
investigated at the identified spouted bed conditions.

Task 5: CFD studies
Evaluate and validate CFD models (two fluid model (TFM)) (using Fluent and/or CFX (Du et al., 2006)
and the discrete particle model (DPM) (Link, 2006; Link et al., 2004), or as it is also called, the discrete
element method (DEM) (Limtrakul et al., 2004)). Validate the available closures (including particle-
particle interaction forces, thermal expansion of the gas phase, by incorporating the energy balance
equation into the model (Antal et al., 2004)) against the detailed hydrodynamic parameters obtained as
benchmark data in Tasks 2, 3 and 4.
Use the validated CFD model(s) and closures to assess the proposed scale-up methodology for
hydrodynamic similarity with the change of conditions and scales. This success will allow for the use of
CFD models to look for the conditions and scales that ensure detailed hydrodynamic similarity by
searching different conditions to maintain similarity in the proposed scale-up parameters (solids holdup
distribution or radial profile, dimensionless spout diameter, and dimensionless fountain height). Compare
the findings with those obtained with dimensionless groups as scaling parameters as evaluated in Tasks
3.2 and 3.4
Work Accomplished:
The two most commonly used methods for modeling gas–solids two-phase systems are the
discrete element method (DEM) and the two fluid model method (TFM). In the DEM approach, the gas
phase is described by a locally averaged Navier–Stokes equation, the motion of individual particles is
traced, and the two phases are coupled by interphase forces. For the TFM approach, the different phases
are mathematically treated as interpenetrating continua, and the conservation equation for each of the two
phases is derived to obtain a set of equations that have similar structure for each phase. Both of the two
approaches are adopted in spouted bed modeling. Krzywanski et al. (1992) developed a multi-
dimensional model to describe the gas and particle dynamic behavior in a spouted bed. Kawaguchi et al.
(2000) proposed an Eulerian–Lagrangian approach, the three-dimensional motion of solids was discretely
traced by solving Newton’s equation of motion using the DEM. Huilin et al. (2001) presented a two-fluid
gas–solids flow model for spouted beds, viewing spout and annulus as two interconnected regions. Huilin
et al. (2004) also incorporated a kinetic-frictional constitutive model for dense assemblies of solids in the
simulation of spouted beds. The model treated the kinetic and frictional stresses of particles additively.
From the point of view of computation, the TFM approach is much more feasible for practical
applications to complex multiphase flows; therefore extensive attention has been given to improving its
accuracy. The success of the TFM depends on the proper description of the solid stress and the interfacial
forces. By introducing the concepts of solid “pressure” and “viscosity”, the well-known granular kinetic
theory has been well established and now has been widely employed for the solid stress calculation. The
interfacial forces include drag force, lift force and virtual mass force, etc. In coupling the equations of the
two phases, due to the large difference in their densities, those forces other than drag force are less
significant, and thus can be usually neglected.
Consequently, in most reports, whenever the interfacial forces were dealt with, only drag force
was considered. Some authors had noticed that the choice of drag models played a critical role in
simulating gas–solids two-phase flows. Yasuna et al. (1995) showed that the solution of their model was
sensitive to the values of drag coefficients. O’Brien and Syamlal (1993) suggested that the drag force
correlations for fine particles should be corrected to account for the formation of clusters. Enwald et al.
(1996) found that the predictions based on different drag models were in good agreements with each other
for the dilute region, but obviously different for the dense region. Van Wachem et al. (2001) noticed that
flow predictions were not sensitive to the use of different solid stress models or radial distribution
functions, as the different approaches were very similar in dense flow regimes, but the application of
different drag models significantly impacted the flow of the solid phase. More or less, these results
signify that an improper choice of drag models may yield inaccurate results or even lead to incorrect
descriptions of gas–solids two-phase flows. Du et al. (2006) studied the effect of different drag models on
spouted bed simulation and found that Gidaspow (1994) giving the best fits to the experimental data.
80

<!-- page 81 -->

Since the drag force is the only accelerating force acting on particles, thus the selection of drag models
make difference in the CFD simulation of the spouted beds.
Two–fluid model (TFM) is applied to the modeling of complicated gas–solids flow in spouted
beds. By TFM approach, the gas–solid two phases are treated mathematically as continuous and fully
interpenetrating. Generalized Navier–Stokes equations are used for the interacting continua. To close the
governing equations, the constitutive relations are needed. Because the solid phase is treated as
continuous, it has similar properties to a continuous fluid. By using the kinetic theory of granular flows
(Ding and Gidaspow, 1990), the viscous forces and the pressure of solid phase can be described as a
function of the granular temperature (Lun et al., 1984). The stress of solid phase due to frictional
interactions between particles is represented by using the Schaeffer (1987) model. The governing
equations and constitutive relations for spouted beds are listed below.
In spouted beds, the gases and particles in the spout region rise at high velocities, while particles
move slowly downwards in the annulus region between the spout and the wall. In the spout region, the
influence of gas turbulent fluctuations on overall gas–solid flow behavior is greatly essential. However,
there has been no consistency on whether turbulent fluctuation effects should be considered and which
turbulent model is the most suitable for CFD simulation of spouted beds. Du et al. (2006c) applied the
dispersed turbulence model and the per-phase turbulence model, respectively, to simulate the flow in
spouted beds. The results showed that the dispersed turbulence model could predict reasonable trends of
spouting flow, while the per-phase turbulence model overestimated the particle turbulent fluctuations and
could not predict the spouting flow trends. Hence, in the present work, the dispersed turbulence model has
been adopted, where turbulence predictions for gas phase are obtained by the standard k–ε model
supplemented with extra terms that include the inter-phase turbulent momentum transfer.
As mentioned above, the gas–solid drag coefficient is critical to a successful simulation, and the
Gidaspow model, a combination of the Wen and Yu model (1966) and the Ergun equation (1952), can
reasonably describe the interaction of gas and solid phases in spouted beds. Hence, the Gidaspow model
has been applied, and its expressions are also summarized in the section below.

                Numerical Simulation Method. The simulations of spouted beds have been carried out with the
       CFD package FLUENT 6.3. The set of governing equations mentioned in section above have been solved
       by a finite control volume technique. The Phase Coupled SIMPLE algorithm, which is an extension of the
       SIMPLE algorithm for multiphase flow, has been used for the pressure-velocity coupling and correction.
       The momentum, volume fraction and turbulence equations have been discretized by a first-order upwind
       scheme.
                Two-dimensional axial symmetry has been assumed in the simulation. The dimensions of the
       computational domain in radial and axial directions are the same as those of the actual spouted bed. Grids
       have been created in a CAD program GAMBIT 2.4 and imported into FLUENT 6.3. The geometry of
       spouted bed and its grids are shown in Figure 82. A transient simulation has been adopted, using a very
       small time step of 0.0001 sec with about 20 iterations per time step. A convergence criterion of 10-3 for
       each scaled residual component has been specified for the relative error between two successive
       iterations.




                                                                                  	
                                                       83

<!-- page 84 -->

Figure 82. Schematic of geometry of grids for spouted bed
Grid Convergence Studies. Adaptive Mesh Refinement (AMR) study is done for the spouted
bed simulations in the present study. AMR is a technique for automatically refining (or de-refining)
certain region of the physical domain in a finite difference calculation. For time-dependent calculations,
the time step as well as the grid spacing may also be a function of the level of refinement. The
hierarchical structured grid approach now known as AMR was first developed by Berger and Oliger
(1984) for hyperbolic partial differential equations. The approach to adaptive gridding used here was
developed for conservation laws and demonstrated to be highly successful for gas dynamics by Berger
and Colella (1989) in two dimensions. Bell, Berger, Saltzman and Welcome (1991) extended the
methodology to three dimensions. More recently, AMR has been extended to a variety of problems and
algorithm choices, including, but not limited to, solving the variable coefficient Poisson equation,
Helmholtz equation, system of hyperbolic conservation laws governing inviscid gas dynamics,
compressible and incompressible Navier-Stokes equations, and the equations that govern reacting flows,
such as those that occur in premixed and non-premixed combustion.
An initial grid hierarchy is created at the start of any calculation, based on the initial data. As the
simulation progresses, the grids may dynamically change to reflect the evolving solution. In both cases,
the same procedures are used to create new grids. Construction of the grid hierarchy is based on error
estimation criteria specified by the user to indicate where additional resolution is required. Cells requiring
additional refinement at a given level are identified and tagged using these criteria. Error estimation may
use Richardson extrapolation as described in Berger and Colella (1984), or it may use some other user-
supplied criteria. In the present study Richardson extrapolation criterion is used. The tagged cells are then
84

*[figure(s) on p.84 → figures/scale-up-of-triso-fuel-coaters-via-advanced-measurement-and-computational-techni-p84.png]*


<!-- page 85 -->

grouped into rectangular patches using the clustering algorithm given in Berger and Rigoustsos (1991).
The generated patches will, in general, contain cells that were not tagged for refinement. The grid
efficiency is the fraction of the cells in a new grid that are tagged by the error estimation process. A grid
efficiency criterion (typically 70%) determines the minimum grid efficiency that is acceptable. These
rectangular patches are refined to form the grids at the next level. The process is repeated until either the
error criteria are satisfied or a user-specified maximum level of refinement is reached. The proper nesting
requirement is imposed at this stage.
The geometry of Spouted bed used for our investigations on scale-up is shown below (0.152 m
spouted Bed):
Figure 83. Schematic of spouted bed geometry used in the present study
The spout diameter and fountain height are important characteristic parameters which can be used
to determine how well numerical models capture the hydrodynamic behavior of spouted beds. Three
different grids (Figure 84) which satisfy the Richardson extrapolation criterion were selected: 36*186,
42*216 and 26*135.
The predicted spout diameters for all three grid resolutions are almost the same. The predicted
fountain height from medium grid resolution (36*186) is close to the height from finer grid resolution
((26*135), but higher than the height from coarse grid resolution (26*135). Radial profiles of voidage
(Figure 84) and particle velocity (Figure 85. and 86.) in the spout and annulus region at H = 0.118 m and
H = 0.168 m were compared to further study the grid dependence. The results do not differ vastly except
for the coarse grid resolution (26*135). Nearly grid independent results can usually be obtained from
medium grid resolution. Thus keeping in mind the accuracy and computational time the simulations for
spouted beds can be performed mainly using the medium grid resolution (36*186), which also satisfies
the Richardson extrapolation criterion.

3-D and 2-D Simulation Studies for Spouted Bed. The simulations for spouted bed were done
for 3 dimensional (3D) models in order to compare the results with 2 dimensional (2D) models, obtained
from both types of geometry. Meshes were created by the CAD program of GAMBIT 2.2.30 and
exported into Fluent 6.3.26. The mesh size of 5% larger than the particle diameter was equidistant in all
directions. The Phase Coupled SIMPLE algorithm was used for the pressure-velocity coupling and
correction. A first-order upwind differencing scheme for momentum and volume fraction variables was
used. Because of the usual instability and convergence for multiphase simulation, a very small time step
(0.0001 s) with about 20 iterations per time step was used. A convergence criterion of 10-3 for each
scaled residual component was specified for the relative error between two successive iterations. Meshes
for 2D model incorporated axisymmetric models for convenience of simulation and also to reduce
simulation time. The results for 2D and 3D simulations are presented below (Figures 89 and 90).

conditions then we will perform additional 3D simulation.

Influence of wall boundary conditions on CFD simulation of spouted beds. The influence of
solid-phase wall boundary condition on the flow behavior of spouted beds was investigated using TFM
approach in the CFD software FLUENT 6.3. Parametric studies of specularity coefficient and particle–
wall restitution coefficient were performed to evaluate their impact on the flow hydrodynamics in terms
of fountain height, spout diameter, pressure drop, local voidage and particle velocity. The numerical
predictions were compared with available experimental data in the literatures to obtain the suitable values
of specularity coefficient and particle–wall restitution coefficient for spouted beds.
Initial and boundary conditions. Appropriate initial and boundary conditions of velocities,
pressure, and granular temperature are crucial to solving the equations listed in Table 1. The simulations
start from a static bed. Particles are lying in the spouted bed with maximum packing limit and the static
89

*[figure(s) on p.89 → figures/scale-up-of-triso-fuel-coaters-via-advanced-measurement-and-computational-techni-p89.png]*


<!-- page 90 -->

bed height is equal to the fixed bed height of the experiment. The velocities of gas and solid phase are set
to be zero. At the inlet, the uniform distribution is assumed for velocity components, kinetic energy of
turbulence and energy dissipation rate of gas phase. Gas is injected only in the axial direction, and solids
velocity is zero. The turbulent kinetic energy of gas phase,K =1.5(V I)2, where I is the turbulent
g g
intensity and is set as 10%. The turbulent dissipation rate of gas phase,ε =C 3/4(K 1.5 /l), where
g µ g
l=0.07D . At the outlet, the pressure is set at an ambient atmosphere. At the axis of spouted bed, the
h
velocity gradients for two phases and the granular temperature gradient of particles along the radial
direction are zero.
At the wall, the gas tangential and normal velocities are set to be zero. The normal velocity of
particles is also set at zero. The Johnson and Jackson (1987) wall boundary condition is applied for the
tangential velocity and granular temperature of solid phase at the wall. As expressed in Eqs. (74) – (76),
the slip velocity between particles and the wall can be obtained by equating the tangential force exerted
on the boundary and the particle shear stress close to the wall. Similarly, the granular temperature at the
wall is obtained by equating the granular temperature flux at the wall to the inelastic dissipation of
energy, and to the generation of granular energy due to slip at the wall region.

where φ is the specularity coefficient, which specifies the fraction of collisions which transfer
momentum to wall. e is the particle–wall restitution coefficient, which describes the amount of
w
dissipation of the solids turbulent kinetic energy with the wall by collisions.
As mentioned above, particle–wall restitution coefficients have a significant effect on the flow
behavior. The coefficients are difficult to measure, and different values have been used in the open
literatures on CFD simulation of gas–particle flow systems without a clear explanation of their choices.
To evaluate the impact on the flow behavior in spouted beds and obtain optimal values, different particle–
wall restitution coefficients have been investigated in the present work. Three particle–wall restitution
coefficient values (e = 0.8, 0.9, and 0.99) will be used respectively. Conditions and parameters for the
w
numerical simulation of spouted beds are summarized in Table 9.
90

<!-- page 91 -->

Table 9. Conditions for the numerical simulation of spouted bed
Effect of specularity coefficient. In the Johnson and Jackson boundary condition, the specularity
coefficient, φ, characterizes the friction between particles and wall. For φ = 0, a free-slip boundary
condition without frictional effect of particles on the wall is used, while when φ = 1, a no-slip boundary
condition with frictional effect of particles on the wall is employed. Low φ represents the high-slip
boundary condition with small frictional effect of particles on the wall. There are few experimental data
reported in the open literatures. During the CFD simulation of gas-solid flow systems, the specularity
coefficient is suggested to be adjusted to satisfy the experimental data (Benyahia et al. 2007). To
investigate the influence of specularity coefficient on the flow behavior in spouted beds, five cases with
different specularity coefficient values (φ = 0, 0.01, 0.05, 0.1, 0.2, and 1) were simulated.
Figure 91 shows the simulated solid volume fraction distribution in spouted beds using different
φ. The typical flow pattern of spouted beds including three regions, the spout in the center, the fountain
above the bed surface, and the annulus between the spout and the wall, are clearly observed. The solid
volume fraction is low in the spout, while the volume fraction is high in the annulus. However, somewhat
different fountain heights and spout shapes are obtained for different φ. The fountain heights decrease
slightly with the increasing of φ. As shown in Table 10, the fountain height is the highest for φ = 0. When
φ =0, there is no friction between particles and wall, and the particles can slip freely on the wall. None of
the energy carried by the entering gas is dissipated to prevent the friction between particles and wall, and
all the energy is just used to drive the particles circulating between the spout and annulus region, leading
to a high fountain height. The spout shape for φ = 1, corresponding to a no-slip condition at the wall,
shows the greatest deviation from the others. For the no-slip boundary condition, there is a strong friction
between particles and wall, which prevents the particles downward flow, resulting in a low particle
circulation rate. The action region of the entering gas extends because of less particles circulating to the
entrance of spouted beds. Hence, the spout diameter near the entrance for φ = 1 is noticeably bigger than
other cases.

Specularity Co-efficient, φ 0 0.01 0.05 0.2 1
Fountain Height, m 0.596 0.590 0.584 0.572 0.560

91

*[figure(s) on p.91 → figures/scale-up-of-triso-fuel-coaters-via-advanced-measurement-and-computational-techni-p91.png]*


<!-- page 92 -->

Figure 91: Predicted solid volume fraction distribution for different specularity coefficients.
The specularity coefficient characterizes the friction between particles and wall. A strong friction
results in a high wall shear stress. Table 10 compares the wall shear stress at different φ. For φ = 0, there
is no friction between the particles and wall, so the shear stress is zero. For φ = 1, the particles stick to the
wall, the shear stress is the maximum. With increasing φ, the wall shear stress increases significantly. The
maximal shear stress reaches to 122 Pa in the present simulation. The difference of wall shear stress has
an effect on the flow behavior of spouted beds. The higher the wall shear stress is, the harder the particles
near the wall move downward, resulting in a lower fountain height, as shown in Figure 91.
In spouted beds, the overall pressure drop is balanced by the particles weight and wall shear. The
specularity coefficient affects the wall shear stress, consequently, it has an influence on the overall
pressure drop. Table 10 compares the pressure drop at different φ. It is clear that the pressure drops
predicted at different φ show significant differences. With increasing φ, the wall shear stress caused by
downward solid flow increases, and the pressure drop decreases. For the free-slip boundary condition, the
wall shear stress is zero, so the pressure drop is the highest, while for the no-slip condition, the wall shear
stress is maximum and the pressure drop is lowest. The significant effect of specularity coefficient on
pressure drop suggests that it is very important to adapt suitable specularity coefficient for CFD
simulation of spouted beds.
Figure 92 presents the radial profiles of particle velocity in the spout and annulus region of
spouted beds for different specularity coefficients, respectively. There is a minor difference of particle
velocity in the spout region, while a pronounced difference in the annulus region for different φ. A
smaller specularity coefficient leads to the lower particle velocities both in the spout and annulus region.
When φ =0, no-slip boundary condition, the wall shear stress prevents the particles to move downward,
resulting in a lower velocity. When φ =1, the particles can freely slip on the wall, so the particles have a
higher velocity. It can be clearly observed in Figures 92 (a)–(d) that the deviation of particle velocities for
92

*[figure(s) on p.92 → figures/scale-up-of-triso-fuel-coaters-via-advanced-measurement-and-computational-techni-p92.png]*


<!-- page 93 -->

different φ increases with the radial distance, indicating the effect of specularity coefficient on particle
velocities is becoming pronounced. Close to the wall, the difference of particle velocities reaches the
maximum due to the strong effect of wall shear stress. Comparing the particle velocity profiles at
different heights in the annulus region (Figures 93 (a)–(d)), it is also found that the effect of specularity
coefficient on particle velocity spreads out with the increase of bed height.
Figure 92: Radial profiles of particle velocity for different specularity coefficients in the spout region at
different bed heights.

Figure 94. Radial profiles of voidage in the spout region for different specularity coefficients
The radial profiles of voidage in the spout region of spouted beds for different specularity
coefficients are shown in Figure 94. The profiles are little different except for φ = 1, which is in
accordance with the observation of Figure 92. A combination of Figures 92, 93 and 94, it is interesting to
find that the results for φ = 1 deviate most extensively from the others. However, most CFD simulations
of spouted beds (Du et al. 2006; Zhonghua and Mujumdar, 2008; Bettega et al. 2009a; Duarte et al. 2009;
Gryczka et al. 2009; Shuyan et al. 2009; Dan et al. 2010.) used the no-slip (φ = 1) wall boundary
conditions. The simulation results need to be further verified.
Effect of particle–wall restitution coefficient. For the numerical simulation of dense gas–solid
flows, the particle–wall restitution coefficient, e , describes the amount of the dissipation by collisions of
w
solids turbulent kinetic energy with the wall. A value of e close to unity implies very low dissipation of
w
granular energy at the wall. The value of 0.8–1 is commonly used. So, the influence of the particle–wall
restitution coefficient on the flow behavior of spouted beds was evaluated by comparing the simulated
results for e = 0.8, 0.9 and 0.99. Figure 95 shows the simulated solid volume fraction distribution in
w
spouted beds using different e . Different particle–wall restitution coefficients give the similar flow
w
pattern. When the fountain height, wall shear stress, and pressure drop are compared for different e , as
w
shown in Table 11, no pronounced changes are observed.
Table 11. Fountain height, wall shear stress and pressure drop for different particle–wall restitution
coefficients
95

*[figure(s) on p.95 → figures/scale-up-of-triso-fuel-coaters-via-advanced-measurement-and-computational-techni-p95.png]*


<!-- page 96 -->

.
Figure 95. Predicted solid volume fraction distribution for different particle–wall restitution coefficients
As shown in Figure 95, the flow patterns of spouted beds are similar at different e . The particle–
w
wall restitution coefficient hardly influences the flow in the spout region. So, more interest is focused on
the investigation of the flow behavior in annulus region. Figure 96 shows the predicted granular
temperature near the wall for different e . The granular temperature rises with increasing e . A lower
w w
particle–wall restitution coefficient causes more dissipation of the particle fluctuation energy at the wall,
leading to the decrease of granular temperature. Similar results were also reported by Natarajan and Hunt
(1998). However, for the e values tested in the current study, the influence of particle–wall restitution
w
coefficient on the granular temperature profile is fairly minor.
Figure 96. Granular temperature near the wall for different particle-wall restitution coefficients.
96

*[figure(s) on p.96 → figures/scale-up-of-triso-fuel-coaters-via-advanced-measurement-and-computational-techni-p96.png]*


<!-- page 97 -->

Figure 97. Particle velocity profiles for different particle-wall restitution coefficients.
When the particle velocity profiles in the annulus region are examined, as shown in Figure 97, it
is found that varying the wall restitution values does not affect the particle velocities significantly. The
differences of particle velocity at different e are negligible when compared to the differences at different
w
φ. It can be concluded that the particle–wall restitution coefficient plays only a minor role in the
numerical modeling of gas–solid flow in spouted beds. The current results are consistent with the
observations by Neri and Gidaspow (2000), McKeen and Pugsley (2003), and Almuttahar and Taghipour
(2008). Neri and Gidaspow (2000) examined two wall restitution coefficients of 0.96 and 0.8 for CFB
riser, and reported that the wall restitution coefficient had some effect on the solid concentration near the
wall, but its value was not critical for the overall definition of the flow pattern.
Comparison with experimental data. According to the simulation results, the wall boundary
condition has a significant effect on the flow behavior of spouted beds, and using a suitable boundary
condition is critical for giving accurate prediction. However, the parameters of boundary condition are
hard to be measured by experiments. So the simulated results with different wall boundary conditions
should be compared with experimental data to determine the suitable parameters. Comparing the
predicted fountain heights and pressure drops in Tables 10 and 11, it is clear that the specularity
coefficient has a more pronounced effect on the pressure drop than on the fountain height, while the
97

*[figure(s) on p.97 → figures/scale-up-of-triso-fuel-coaters-via-advanced-measurement-and-computational-techni-p97.png]*


<!-- page 98 -->

particle–wall restitution coefficient plays only a small role. The fountain height and bed pressure drop
obtained by the experiment of He et al. (1994a, b) was 0.25m and 3.00 kPa, respectively. Comparing the
simulated results at different boundary conditions to the experimental data, the value of φ = 0.05 and
e =0.9 provides the best agreement with experimental data.
w
Figure 98. Comparison of experimental data and predicted results
In order to further validate if φ = 0.05 and e =0.9 is suitable for the modeling of spouted beds, the
w
predicted particle velocity and voidage profiles at H = 0.168m and 0.268 m are compared with
experimental measurements in Figure 98. The simulated particle velocities are in a good agreement with
experimental data, while the voidages are slightly different from those measured. Considering the error
produced by the measurement, we think that the deviation is acceptable. Hence, it is suggested that φ=
0.05 and e =0.9 is suitable for the CFD modeling of spouted beds.
w
TFM approach has been used to investigate the hydrodynamics of spouted beds at different wall
boundary conditions. Several cases with different specularity coefficient values (φ = 0, 0.01, 0.05, 0.1,
0.2, and 1) and different particle–wall restitution coefficient values (e = 0.8, 0.9 and 0.99) were
w
simulated to evaluate their impact on the flow behavior of spouted beds. The spout diameter, fountain
height, pressure drop, local voidage and particle velocity obtained from different cases were compared.
The simulated results show that the solid-phase wall boundary condition plays an important role in the
CFD modeling of spouted beds. The specularity coefficient has an insignificant effect on the fountain
height, spouted diameter, the solid velocity and volume fraction distribution in the spouting region, but a
pronounced effect on the wall shear stress, pressure drop, and the solid velocity distribution in the annulus
region. The influence of wall specularity coefficient on the flow in the annulus region is greater than that
98

*[figure(s) on p.98 → figures/scale-up-of-triso-fuel-coaters-via-advanced-measurement-and-computational-techni-p98.png]*


<!-- page 99 -->

in the spout region. The results obtained from no-slip wall boundary condition deviate most extensively
from the others. The particle–wall restitution coefficient plays only a minor role in the modeling of gas–
solid flow in spouted beds. The predicted results were compared with available experimental data in the
literature to obtain the suitable values of specularity coefficient and particle–wall restitution coefficient
for spouted beds.
CFD Validation for Selected Models. In the present work, the experimental data obtained by He
et al. (1997) was used to validate the simulation results for spouted beds. He et al. (1997) did not measure
the local hydrodynamics of particle velocity and solids holdup in the spouted bed, but evaluated the
global parameters, such as fountain height and spout diameter. He et al. (1994a; 1994b) had measured the
radial profiles of particles velocity and solids holdup in another spouted bed using a fiber optic probe.
These experimental data were used to validate the CFD model for spouted beds by several researchers
(Huilin et al., 2004; Du et al, 2006a; 2006b; Zhonghua et al. 2008; Bettega et al., 2009; Duarte et al.
2009). Hence, the available experimental data of the qualitative profiles of particles velocity and solids
holdup as well as quantitative global parameters have been combined to validate the CFD model for
spouted beds.
The conditions used to simulate the spouted bed are listed in Table 12. Figure 99 shows the
simulated particles velocity vector and solids volume fraction of Case A, where the arrow and color of the
vectors represent the motion direction and magnitude of particles velocities, respectively. The typical
flow pattern of spouted beds including three regions, the spout in the center, the fountain above the bed
surface, and the annulus between the spout and the wall are clearly observed. Solid particles are carried
up by the gas in the spout, reach to the bed surface and form a fountain. Particles move upwards in the
center of the fountain until the top, and then fall into the annulus because of gravity. Particles move
slowly in the annulus, and finally flow from the annulus to the spout at the entrance region. The particles
motion in the spout, fountain and annulus regions forms a circulation of particles in the spouted bed. The
solids volume fraction is low and particle velocity is high in the spout, while the solids volume fraction is
high and particle velocity is low in the annulus. It can also be seen that near the upper part of the spout
and the center of the fountain, there exists a somewhat denser zone surrounding the spout axis. This
phenomenon was also observed in the experiments of Grace and Mathur (1978) and He et al. (1994a), and
the simulation results of Du et al. (2006a) and Zhonghua et al. (2009).
Figure 100 presents the simulated and experimental radial profiles of particle velocity and gas
phase holdup (voidage) at different bed heights. There is similar distributions between simulated and
experimental particle velocities and gas phase holdup, although their magnitudes are not identical due to
using different operating conditions. Table 13 shows the spout diameters and fountain heights of Case A.
The simulated spout diameters and fountain heights for Case A are in agreement with the experimental
results of He et al. (1997).

Scale-up of Spouted Beds
         Assessment of Dimensionless Group Approach. He et al. (1997) designed some matched
(similar values of dimensionless groups in two different spouted beds) and mismatched (dissimilar values
of dimensionless groups in two different spouted beds) experiments to verify their scaling parameters for
spouted beds. Among their cases, Cases A and B were matched and designed to study the validity of the
scaling relationships. Cases C - F were used to examine the influence of each dimensionless group on
similarity, one or more groups were purposely mismatched. In the present work, Cases A - E have been
chosen to assess the ability of dimensionless analysis methodology for spouted beds scale-up using CFD
approach. The parameters of Cases A - E used by He et al. (1997) in their experimental investigation are

Match and mismatch conditions were studied using computational fluid dynamics. The different
measurement levels studied is shown in Figure 101. Figure 102 represents the solid hold-up profile and
dimensionless particle velocity profile in 0.152 m and 0.076 m spouted beds respectively for the match
and mismatch conditions. Comparison of the profiles for all the cases clearly shows that the profiles are
not similar using the conditions proposed by He et al. (1997).
Figure 102 a, b and c; represents the solids holdup profiles. The maximum variation of solids
holdup is in the spout region. This is caused by the jet of gas, which carries the particles up and then falls
back onto the bed surface due to gravity. The solids movement into the spout region from the annulus
varies at different heights of the spouted bed. Two zones where there is maximum inflow of solids to the
spout region from the annulus are at the inlet of the spouted bed and at the neck region formed by the
spout. However, there is inflow of solids from the annulus to the spout region along the height of the
spouted bed. The solids volume fraction is low near the inlet of the spouted bed even though there is
maximum inflow of solids from annulus to spout region because the solids is carried by the gas whose
velocity is maximum at the inlet. The solids flowing into the spout region gets carried away by the
incoming gas immediately. At higher regions of the spouted bed there is high volume fraction of solids as
the velocity of the gas carrying the solids decreases. This explains the variation in the solids volume
fraction at different heights of spouted bed.
The annulus represents a slowly moving packed bed, where there is dense packing of solids. Due
to this, the solids volume fraction remains almost the same at different heights of the spouted bed. When
comparing the different scaled spouted beds, much attention is given to the spout region because it
dictates the spouted bed hydrodynamics. When the solid holdup profiles are compared for the different
cases of spouted bed suggested by He et al. (1997), there is a big difference (absolute deviation = 23%) in
the spout region between the reference case A and the different match (case B) and mismatch (case C, D
and E) cases. Matching condition is much more important due to scaling reasons. The absolute deviation
in this case is 21%, which is considered to be large in engineering terms.
Figure 101. Schematic representation of different axial measurement levels performed on spouted bed.
Particle velocity is another important parameter in the scaling process. Due to the nature of the
gas phase in the spouted bed, the particle velocity varies along the height. The gas phase (air) enters the
bed at the bottom, penetrating the solid bed of particles. The velocity of the gas is high at the inlet and it
decreases as it moves up the spouted bed. Hence, the solids velocity is high at the inlet of spouted bed and
decreases as it moves up the spouted bed. In the annulus region, since the solids are moving down and the
solids are dense, the solids velocity is low and negative. The magnitude of the solids velocity in the
annulus region is not high, but very low. Due the contrasting difference of solids velocity in these two
regions, the comparison of this parameter in the spout region is important.

parameters (solids volume fraction and particle velocity) are significantly apart, but also the global
parameters which were shown to be close in a half cylindrical spouted beds by He et al. (1997).

Comparison of all the above parameters has shown that the deviations between the reference case
(case A) and the different match and mismatch cases are significant. The matching of hydrodynamic
profiles is important when scaling of spouted beds is required. Based on the above-mentioned analysis of
the results, the following limitations of the current dimensionless groups approach can be explained.
Limitations of the current dimensionless group scale-up approach are:
1. The deviation between the local parameter profiles is significantly large.
2. The annulus of spouted bed has similar profiles for all the cases due to the nature of the
zone. Since the annulus consists of slowly moving packed bed and due to dense solids
phase accumulation this result is quiet obvious.
3. The spout region has large deviations due to the variation of gas phase velocity along the
height of the spouted bed. The comparison of the profiles in this region leads to large
deviations among the different cases.

4. Assessment of the global parameters (dimensionless spout diameter, dimensionless
fountain height and maximum spoutable bed height) also showed deviations when
simulations were performed in a full cylindrical spouted bed.
Based on the present work, the methodology of dimensional analysis for scale-up of spouted beds
should be modified to establish a reliable scale-up methodology of spouted beds. The new method should
focus on maintaining similar hydrodynamic profiles in spouted bed, which would help in achieving the
desired process.
New Method for Scale-up. Spouted bed is a two-phase system consisting of gas and solids. The
gas phase enters the spouted bed from the bottom as a jet and penetrates the bed of solid particles. As the
gas phase penetrates the solids, the solids are also being carried along with the gas. The gas carrying the
solids, reaches the top of the bed surface forming a fountain at the top. The solids then fall back on to the
bed surface due to gravity. This nature of spouted bed creates three different zones namely: spout, annulus
and the fountain. The gas phase dictates the flow dynamics of the spouted bed. Hence, if the radial profile
or cross sectional distribution of gas holdup is maintained the same, then the two spouted beds would be
similar.
We propose a new hypothesis:
“Radial profile or cross sectional distribution of gas holdup should be the same for two beds to be
hydrodynamically similar”.
If this hypothesis were true, then the first step would be to develop a simple correlation to capture
the radial profiles using the key dimensionless groups.
This work attempts to evaluate the proposed hypothesis utilizing computational fluid dynamics,
which is based on selecting conditions that provides similar radial profiles in spouted beds. This would be
a difficult task as demonstrated earlier as the current dimensionless groups are not enough to predict close
radial profiles of spouted beds. CFD would help in determining the conditions that would provide such
matching radial profiles and hence has been selected to prove the above-mentioned hypothesis.
Several trail simulations were performed using Computational fluid dynamics (CFD) to identify
the conditions, which would give matching radial profiles in two spouted beds. After numerous
simulations following conditions were identified which would give the desired radial profiles. The
conditions are listed in Table 16.

The radial profiles of voidage and dimensionless particle velocity were evaluated for the
conditions mentioned in Table 16. Figure 103 shows the gas holdup (voidage) profiles and Figure 104
represents the dimensionless particle velocity profiles for the two conditions at different heights of the
spouted bed. The gas holdup (profiles) were expected to be same in the annulus region, because the
annulus is a slow moving packed bed with dense concentration of solids. The gas volume fraction is more
or less the same in this zone. Figure 103 reiterates the above statements showing the volume fraction in
the annulus very similar in reference case and similarity condition. The spout region as explained in the
previous section has varying degrees of volume fraction. It was demonstrated that the Cases A and B had
significant difference in the radial profiles in the spout zone. With the new conditions identified the radial
profiles of gas holdup in the spout region were matched in the two spouted beds. Matching of the profiles
in all the three zones of the spouted bed shows the good estimation of the conditions for scaling up.

The particle velocity is another parameter, which needs close examination. Particle velocity in the
annulus is very similar due to the dynamics of the zone. Spout region has different velocities at various
heights of the spouted bed. When comparing two different spouted beds based on particle velocity
profiles it should be first non-dimensionalized. In the present analysis the particle velocities were non-
dimensionalized by dividing them by minimum spouting velocity (U ). When these profiles are
ms
compared in both the spouted beds (Figure 104), it was observed that the radial profiles were matching at
different sections of the spouted bed.

Spout diameters and fountain height (Table 7.6) was evaluated for both the cases using the newly
identified conditions listed in Table 17. The fountain height was determined by gradually increasing the
initial bed height of the spouted bed until there was no spouting obtained in the bed. This simulation
109

results show that the two spouted beds were in close agreement with each other. The dimensionless spout
diameter and dimensionless fountain height were close to the reference case.

The dimensionless pressure profiles were also evaluated (Figure 105) for both spouted beds using
the new conditions. The dimensionless pressure was obtained by dividing the pressure at that particular
point by the overall bed pressure. The plot of the dimensionless pressure along the height of the spouted
bed for both the beds showed a very close resemblance to each other. The conditions listed by He et al.
(1997), when evaluated for dimensionless pressure profiles did not show such close agreement.
The analysis of the results reiterates that the selected conditions were capable of matching the
global (dimensionless spout diameter, dimensionless fountain height and pressure drop profile) and local
parameters (gas holdup and dimensionless particle velocity), which showed that the profiles are very
close to each other in both the spouted beds. The hypothesis of similar radial profile or cross sectional
distribution of gas holdup profiles yields similar hydrodynamics in two spouted beds stands true.
Figure 105. Dimensionless pressure profile for Case A and Case B using new hydrodynamic similarity
conditions determined using CFD simulation
Computational fluid dynamics (CFD) was used to simulate the spouted bed hydrodynamics.
Various models were selected to describe the flow properties and validated against the experimental
works of He et al. (1997). The voidage and particle velocity profiles were in good agreement with the
